                                                 increased 3.6% from the previous
                                                 year, a 33.1% increase in average
                                                 attendance per program.



Our regular program series have had continued loyal participation, including the
Salem Sound Coastwatch ecological lecture series, Claire Keyes' Poetry Salons,
Adult Crafternoons and Craft-and-Chats, and four regular book groups. One of the
highlights of the library’s popular book clubs was a visit with best-selling author,
Dawn Tripp, to discuss her novel Georgia; a Novel of Georgia O'Keeffe. Thanks to
a grant from the Marblehead Cultural Council, we provided a six-week genealogy
program, On the Trail of Your Family Roots, with professional genealogy instructor
Linda B. MacIver. Additional programs included 100 Years of Boston Comedy with
Nick Zaino, Making Medicare Easy, A Perky Pairing: Coffee and Chocolate History
and Tasting, as well as programs in partnership with the Marblehead Conservancy,
the League of Women Voters, and the Marblehead Racial Justice Team.

In addition to programs, we initiated a major cataloging and organization project of
the local historic and archival materials stored in our Marblehead Room, as well as
stocking the Thompson Makerspace with new technologies and supplies to begin
developing the space as a free-use studio. The Carten Gallery opened with a display
of quilts featuring picture book covers created by the Seacoast Quilt Guild.

We also offer a series of Museum Passes (sponsored by The Friends of Abbot Public
Library) and a Library of Things.

CHILDREN’S DEPARTMENT




The Children’s Room serves Marblehead’s youth from birth to age 12 and
their families. Despite being closed for three months during the transition
between buildings, the library offered 205 programs for children attended by
nearly 5,000 patrons.

We offered early literacy programs for ages birth to 5 including Moving &
Grooving, Music with Dara, Stories & Songs with Jeana Khan, Story Time A-Go-
Go and Story Time Yoga. For a wider age range, we offered coloring, crafts, the
Grow It! Seed library sponsored by the Driftwood Garden Club, chess instruction
and a Summer Reading Program. One adult and two teen volunteers assisted with
the craft programs and room decorations.


When the library opened in July 2024, the children’s department was the first to
offer programming for ages 6-12 in the new Thompson Makerspace with a
focus on STEAM topics such as sewing, weather, and Minecraft coding.

Thank you to the following organizations for their children’s program
support: The Oliver P. Killam, Jr. Fund; The Coordinated Family and
Community Engagement Grant of the North Shore; Marblehead Cultural
Council Grants; Driftwood Garden Club; and the Lafayette Nursing Home.
Programs are also made possible through Marblehead residents donating skills
and/or materials, and staff sharing skills.

Abbot Public Library Foundation
We are especially grateful for the Abbot Public Library Foundation (APLF) which is
the separate, not-for-profit, fundraising arm of Abbot Public Library. In addition to
securing private funding to assist in the town-approved renovation, the APLF seeks
to honor the long-time tradition of a public and private partnership to ensure that the
Abbot Public Library continues to serve the Marblehead community as a center of
knowledge, information, and a welcoming gathering place for all.

Managed by a Board of Directors who consult and work in collaboration with the
Abbot Public Library Board of Trustees and the Library Director, the APLF’s Vision
is to empower the Library to continue to update and provide the historic town of

Marblehead with a modern library, a multi-use gathering place, a center of
knowledge, culture and innovation for the community, and to provide an up-to-date,
vital resource for present and future generations. Their mission is to serve as the
fundraising arm of the Library by soliciting and acquiring gifts, grants, endowments,
bequests, and pledges from individuals and organizations for capital improvements
and programs. The goal is to enrich, not replace, traditional tax-based support for the
Library. They are committed to incorporating and upholding the values of
collaboration, innovation, service and inclusion in all aspects of their work.

Second Century Fund
The Board of Trustees gratefully acknowledges the contributions of the Second
Century Fund, an endowment fund that has been dedicated to supporting the Library
since 1994, providing resources that ensure excellence in collections, materials, and
access. As an endowment, the permanent fund generates income to supplement, not
to replace, public support provided by the Town of Marblehead, ensuring that the
Library’s collections meet outstanding levels of breadth and currency that best serve
its citizens. Since its inception, the fund has responded to a variety of librarian
requests and initiatives: a threatening escalation in periodical prices, the first CD-
ROMs, the first DVDs, and the first audio books for young adults, a unique art
encyclopedia, and a need for updated projection/screening equipment, providing
mobile hotspots and Chromebooks for the Library of Things, and the library’s new
self-checkout equipment and digital signage.

The Harold B. and Elizabeth L. Shattuck Memorial Fund
We are grateful to The Harold B. and Elizabeth L. Shattuck Memorial Fund for their
generous contributions for new library carts and Imagination Playground blocks that
will support a new playdate program to be offered by the children’s department.

Friends of Abbot Public Library
The Friends of Abbot Public Library is a volunteer organization dedicated to the
support of Library events and services. We thank them for their support for our
museum pass program, sponsorship of author visits and other adult programs, regular
book sales, and operation of the in-library Sail Away Book Shop.

Driftwood Garden Club
We are grateful to this long-standing garden club for their continued support in
maintaining the Abbot Public Library gardens. We are especially grateful for their
collaboration with the Building Renovation Committee on creating new designs for
the front lawn and the courtyard.

                          BOARD OF ASSESSORS

Under State mandated legislation, the Board of Assessors is responsible for the full
and fair market valuation of real and personal property for the purposes of levying
the property tax as of January 1 every year.

The Board consists of a three-member elected panel with one seat up for renewal
every year. In 2024, Jonathan Lederman was elected for a 3-year term.

The assessing staff performs all duties of the department under the supervision of the
Town Assessor and review of the Board of Assessors.

In determining assessed property values, the primary goal of the Board is to ensure
fair and equitable assessments to all taxpayers. Assessments for the average single-
family dwelling increased 16.7% to $1,202,931 and the median single-family
assessment increased 15% to $928,400. The average single family tax bill increased
4.6% to $10,778 and the median single family tax bill increased 3.0% to $8,318. The
classification hearing held on December 4, 2024, resulted in a single tax rate of $8.96
per thousand dollars of value.

In addition to property valuations, the department also administers statutory
exemptions. Residents who own and occupy their Marblehead property as their
domicile may apply for an exemption as long as they meet the statutory requirements.
Exemptions are available to qualifying disabled veterans, elderly persons, surviving
spouses and blind individuals.

Massachusetts tax law and guidelines for exemptions are constantly changing.
Effective November 2016, under the quarterly tax billing system, the filing deadline
for real and personal property tax abatements is February 1st, (third quarter tax
bill due date) and April 1st for statutory exemptions (three months from the mailing
of the third quarter tax bill).

The Board of Assessor’s granted 144 statutory exemptions for a total of $182,907.62
to qualified applicants and senior work-off volunteers (as of December 2024).

We urge all taxpayers who may have questions regarding abatements or exemptions
to contact the Assessor’s Office at 781-631-0236.

The department is also responsible for excise taxes for boats and motor vehicles.
Annually, the boat owner must certify under the pains and penalties of perjury the
vessel that is listed on their mooring permit. This information is used in conjunction
with the State formula to determine the excise tax amount. For motor vehicles, the



                                         114


                                       115
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

excise tax is based upon the MSRP values provided by the Registry of Motor
Vehicles.

The following 2 tables show the tax dollars raised. The first table is for property and
the second table is for excise.

                            BOARD OF HEALTH

The Board of Health Promotes and Protects the Health, Wellness and Safety of
All Citizens of Marblehead While Ensuring a Clean and Healthy Environment.

A. INTRODUCTION
In the beginning of 2024, the Marblehead Board of Health’s three elected members
were: Helaine R. Hazlett, chair, Joanne Miller, and Tom McMahon. In June, Joanne
Miller stepped down and Tom Massaro, MD, was elected to serve a three-year term.

In 2024, the Marblehead Board of Health met in formal session on seventeen (17)
occasions including Town Meeting.

At the May 2024 Town Meeting, the Board of Health proposed expanding the Board
to 5 members as of Town election 2025. It was unanimously passed by the meeting
attendees.

The Board of Health serves the town in several important ways:
    • It is the primary resource for public health discussions, activities, and policy
       development in the Town.
    • It oversees the solid waste and recycling of the town, the Marblehead
       Transfer Station, and curb side collection.
    • It serves as a local and regional resource. BOH members and staff regularly
       participate in and make valuable contributions to activities, groups, and
       offices in our region, our state, and our nation.

B. PUBLIC HEALTH SERVICES
The Board of Health provides several levels of public health services to the Town. It
manages the traditional disease prevention and control requirements and, at the same
time, is becoming more engaged in health promotion driven by the newly emerging
definition of public health:

“PUBLIC HEALTH IS WHAT WE DO AS A SOCIETY TO CREATE
CONDITIONS IN WHICH EVERYONE CAN BE HEALTHY”
As examples of that evolution in 2024, the Board began planning for a Wellness Fair
and a Substance Abuse Seminar and introduced the concept of developing regular
assessments of the health status of the community.

The Board of Health continues to follow the Massachusetts requirements for both
state and local laws and regulations to perform many critical duties related to the
protection of public health. These duties cover a wide range of public health control




                                        116


                                       117
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

and prevention activities, including disease surveillance; the promotion of sanitary
conditions in housing, recreational facilities, and food establishments; elimination of
nuisances; the protection of the environment; and manages the MMHTF and curbside
collection.

BOH members and staff also attended numerous meetings pertaining to finance,
COVID-19, collaboration, communicable disease, tobacco control, grant application,
sanitation code updates, continuing education, emergency preparedness, mental
health as well as other public health-related issues. Meetings were properly and duly
posted with the Town Clerk.

COVID data show the pandemic phase has ended and the virus is now an endemic
pathogen. The increase in influenza was not surprising since influenza was less
prominent in COVID heavy years.

However, the increase in Pertussis is worrisome. Is this a consequence of vaccine
hesitancy and concerns? To attempt to answer this question the Board will follow the
incidence of Pertussis infections carefully in future years.



                                          117


                                            118
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________


Vaccination remains the best way to prevent pertussis. However, CDC expects
pertussis cases to increase both in unvaccinated and vaccinated populations. Pertussis
sometimes occurs in vaccinated people since the efficacy of the vaccine can decrease
over time.

2. BLOOD PRESSURE SCREENING
BP monitoring is conducted Monday through Friday from 8:00 A.M. to 12:00 noon
at the Board of Health office by appointment only. Additionally, the Public Health
Nurse is at the Marblehead COA on most Thursdays between 11:00 AM-12:00 noon
to conduct a blood pressure clinic.

3. COVID TEST KITS
In 2024, the Board received and distributed COVID-19 Rapid test kits from the
Massachusetts Department of Public Health. A total of 3200 kits were distributed to
Marblehead residents and employees.

The Board of Health did not administer COVID vaccines to the public in 2024. The
MA Department of Public Health provided free vaccine only for uninsured adult
residents in 2024.

4. DISTRIBUTION OF FENTANYL KITS
The Board of Health has distributed over 150 fentanyl test strips this year. Free
fentanyl test strips located outside of the Health Department, one at Fire
Headquarters, and one is located at the Police Station.

6. SANITATION INSPECTION SERVICES
All food service and retail food establishments are regulated by the 2013 Federal Food
Code (with 2015 Amendments). These establishments are required by law and by the
MDPH to be inspected bi-annually. Additionally, walk-through inspections are
conducted at the Board of Health’s discretion. These are a less formal visual
inspection for those establishments about which the Board has received complaints,
those making physical changes, or for the purpose of addressing any type of problem
that may arise during the year.

Establishments Regularly Inspected

7. BEACH AND SWIMMING POOL INSPECTION SERVICES
Beach samples are taken on a weekly basis from June through September for
Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Gas House Beach, Grace Oliver’s Beach and
Stramski Beach. YYY samples were taken during the 2024 beach season.

(Testing is conducted following beach testing standards set forth under 105 CMR
Sect.) Beach water quality must meet both the single sample and the geometric
mean1 (geomean) standards. Single sample results are intended to represent current
conditions while the geomean is intended to reflect typical water quality found during
the preceding time (typically a month).

Public and semipublic pools are monitored for mandatory on-site quality control and
prop management as required by MA 105 CMR 435.000 Minimum Standards for
Swimming Pool.

9. MARBLEHEAD MENTAL HEALTH TASK FORCE
The Marblehead Mental Health Task Force operates under the auspices of, and reports
to, the Board of Health. It convenes as a cross-sector group of mental health providers
and community leaders to ensure that the appropriate intervention strategies are
provided to guide our citizens, particularly the most vulnerable, toward improved
mental health. The Task Force created the website, MARBLEHEADCARES.ORG,
which provides a link to community resources, tools, information and direction to
services to access to support mental health.

The key objectives of the Task Force are to:
1. Provide guidance to support residents in addressing their mental health challenges
2. Promote awareness and understanding to diminish the stigma often associated with
mental health issues and
3. Direct residents to mental health resources and to ensure they achieve appropriate
care.

These goals are designed to foster a supportive environment that enhances the mental
health and overall well-being of the residents of the Town.

C. SOLID WASTE AND RECYCLING

1. THE TRANSFER STATION
The Marblehead Transfer Station is a very significant resource for the community.
Although the decades long transition to become an EPA certified waste management
facility has taken longer than expected, the Town has access to an easy to navigate
space with competent staff available to help with managing household waste and
construction material.

2. WASTE REDUCTION
Marblehead is continually working to maximize waste reduction, recycling and
composting to ensure the safe management, reuse, and disposal of solid and
hazardous waste. It is a difficult task to address the increasing and environmental
cost controls placed on the town by state and federal agencies. The Town needs to
focus on decreasing the amount of waste we generate and increasing the amount
recycled.

     •    Marblehead processed 10,042.34 tons of trash in 2024.
     •    Marblehead recycled a total of 3,067.87 tons of paper, commingled material,
          cardboard, and metal out of the waste stream, realizing diversion savings of
          $360,229.30.
     •    2,500 gallons of waste oil were collected for processing. (This includes
          Town garages.)
     •    24.85 tons of electronics were recycled ethically.
     •    2 Household Hazardous Waste Days were conducted jointly with the Town
          of Swampscott. Materials were collected directly from over 300 households.
     •    180,759 lbs. of textiles/used clothing were recycled through the clothing bin
          program generating $12,653.13.
          2063 mattresses/box springs were recycled.

3.   FOOD AND YARD WASTE DISPOSAL
The Town collects, disposes of and/or composts yard waste and organic
matter. The processing figures for 2024 for organic matter, vegetation, brush,
and wood waste generation are:
     •    Leaves & grass: 16,420 cubic yards
     •    Woodchips (Town Tree Department and power line clearing): 4,280 cubic
          yards.



                                         121


                                            122
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

    •    Brush: 9,020 cubic yards
    •    Curbside Food Waste collected from 751 residents.
    •    Total Food Waste collected: Marblehead Residential, Marblehead School
         and Marblehead Transfer Station – 236.8 tons, which generates diversion
         savings of $27,805.06

4. REFUSE, REDUCE, REUSE, RECYCLE AND ROT
Most Americans are familiar with the phrase “Reduce, Reuse and Recycle.” These
ideas have been promoted over the past decades to reduce the amount of trash and
litter here on planet Earth.

In practice, however, most of the public effort has been focused on encouraging
consumers to recycle. In Massachusetts, there has been an additional focus on
teaching consumers how to recycle correctly and to reduce contamination.
Contamination occurs when non-recycling items get mixed in with recyclables.

Despite good intentions and considerable investments in education, most American
consumers don’t manage household trash and recycling correctly. Most haul our trash
to the curb or place it in a dumpster each week with little thought as to where it goes
or what it costs to dispose of it. Yet each year, consumers, municipalities, and
businesses in Massachusetts spend millions on solid waste disposal fees. Most of this
waste ends up in landfills or incinerators.

According to the Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection,
Marblehead incinerates about 60% of its trash each year while about 40% goes to
landfills. Historically, there were more than 300 operating landfills in Massachusetts.
Today, that number is are down to 6 -all of which are due to reach capacity and close
by 2030. The state now ships approximately 20% of its trash to other states which is
costly and inefficient.

The goal is to move toward a zero-waste system. The Zero Waste International
Alliance (ZWIA) defines zero waste as “the conservation of all resources by means
of responsible production, consumption, reuse, and recovery of products, packaging,
and materials without burning and with no discharges to land, water, or air that
threaten the environment or human health.”

REUSE- Say no to nonessentials.
REDUCE- Adopt minimalist tendencies.
REUSE- Give items a second life.
ROT- Compost all organic waste.




                                         122


                                       123
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Become a smart recycler by following these guidelines:
    • Empty and rinse all food and beverage containers.
    • Remove all plastic and Styrofoam packing from cardboard boxes before
       recycling.
    • Bottles, jars, jugs, and tubs: empty, rinse and replace cap.
   • Ignore the recycling symbol (with a number) that’s on most plastic items –
       it does NOT mean it’s recyclable.
   • Consult the online “Recyclopedia” for recycling answers to over 500 items:
       RecycleSmartMA.org.
   • Place it in loose – Please do not put your recyclables in a plastic bag.
   • Please NO thin-filmed Plastics such as produce bags, packaging, and bubble
       wrap.
   • Wrapping paper and tissue paper are not recyclable

5. COMPOSTING
Composting is the fifth step to achieving zero waste. Even when all actions have
been taken to use your wasted food, certain inedible parts will remain and can be
turned into compost to feed and nourish the soil. Like yard waste, food scraps can
also be composted. Composting these wastes creates a product that can be used to
help improve soils, grow the next generation of crops, and improve water quality.
Food composting could divert as much as 50% of the residential waste stream. For a
small fee, curbside composting is available in Marblehead. Please call the
Marblehead Health Office for more information.

6. SWAP SHED
A temporary Swap Shed is in place until the new one is constructed. The Swap Shop
was established by the Marblehead Board of Health, and it is run by volunteers who
are citizens of Marblehead. The Swap Shed is open on Saturdays from 9-11:30.
During the winter it is only open the first Saturday of the month. The Swap Shed
volunteers

Guidelines for Use of Swap Shed:
Residents must have a current Facility Sticker to use the Swap Shed. No walk-ins are
permitted.

Items must be approved by an attendant.
Items must be functional and have all parts.
Area in front of the Swap Shop is for short term parking – 15 minutes
Items left there are intended for Reuse – Not Resale

D. A RESOURCE FOR COMMUNITY AND REGION.
The Board of Health serves as an important hub on health issues for the town and the
region. The Board of Health has also been on the cutting edge of adopting policies
and regulations, including organic pest management for Town owned properties, by
banning tobacco use on all public properties, banning idling of buses and delivery
trucks, and recommended at Town meeting banning the use of plastic bags in markets
and shops. The Board of Health works with other agencies to address regional health
challenges:

There are two nonprofits which work directly with the Board and receive
funding for services provided in partnership:

1. MARBLEHEAD COUNSELING CENTER
In its 56th year, the Marblehead Counseling Center (MCC) continues to strengthen its
collaboration with the Board of Health to provide the Town with counseling and
community social services. These comprehensive services provide a unique
opportunity to any resident or Town Employee regardless of their ability to pay a fee.
Clients work with our experienced, dedicated clinicians to address a myriad of
challenges and diagnoses. Additionally, wraparound social services are provided to
enhance household stability and create connections to essential resources that help to
sustain long-term health and wellness. During fiscal year 2025, MCC provided direct
therapy to 141 different individuals who live or work in Marblehead providing 4507
sessions for individual, couple, and family counseling and social services. This year
MCC brought the community together for several meaningful events, honoring our
founders and raising awareness of the Counseling Center’s mission. We elevated our
profile through a multi-pronged strategy to ease the process of connecting community
members to support. Through our new website, social media outreach, community
presentations, and more we aimed to reinforce a steady presence as a partner to those
seeking assistance. While the year was marked by celebrations of MCC’s history and
legacy, the immense need for quality local mental health services has not abated. New
intakes have in fact surpassed pandemic-era levels and complex, trauma-informed



                                        124


                                       125
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

interventions for clients of all ages require specialized clinical skills and extended
lengths of treatment. MCC’s staff persists in rising to meet these needs. The
Marblehead community continues to rely on seeking aid at the doorstep of the
Marblehead Counseling Center to address the critical needs that place great strain on
local households. Last year, over 200 hours were dedicated to connecting such
households to emergency financial assistance, eviction diversion protection, utility
stabilization and more through MCC’s social service program. With the generous
support of the Marblehead Female Humane Society, a part-time social service case
manager helps to carry out these efforts to shore up resources to help our neighbors
afford to live with dignity while navigating the ever-increasing costs of living. The
Counseling Center continues to collaborate with community organizations to enrich
the network in providing programming and responsive services for a healthier
Marblehead. Working locally with the Marblehead Mental Health Task Force, the
Ministerial Association, the schools, first responders, local physicians and more
ensures a seamless, no-wrong-door approach to accessing assistance. Additional
regional partnerships with prevention planning, adult education, financial coaching,
and career development services have further expanded avenues toward longer term
sustainability of community wellness.

2. HAWC (Healing Abuse Working for Change)
The mission of HAWC is to create social change by acting against personal and
societal patterns of violence and oppression. HAWC strives to help abused women,
children, men, and nonbinary people live free from violence and fear. As a leading
domestic violence agency in Massachusetts, HAWC provides a domestic violence
helpline, emergency shelter, legal services, advocacy, and counseling services to
more than 2,500 families in the North Shore each year. HAWC understands the
complexity of abuse, and has expanded its language capacity so abused women, men,
nonbinary people, LGBTQ+ individuals, older adults, undocumented individuals, and
people with disabilities can feel comfortable accepting its services.
The Board of Health also participates in several state collaboratives and
consortia to participate in efforts to improve the overall health of the
Commonwealth. These include:

1. NORTH SHORE/CAPE ANN TOBACCO PROGRAM
The North Shore Tobacco Control Program strives to change community and social
norms regarding the use of tobacco products by supporting proven public health
efforts and initiatives to reduce the use of tobacco. This is accomplished by raising
public awareness about tobacco issues, such as youth access and secondhand smoke,
through prevention, intervention, policies, and enforcement initiatives.

The program is funded through the Massachusetts Tobacco Control Program (MTCP)
of the Department of Public Health and is part of Smoke Free Solutions North, the



                                        125


                                           126
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

MTCP Community Mobilization Network. MTCP was created through a statewide
referendum in 1992, which placed a 25-cent per pack tax on cigarettes and smokeless
tobacco products.

In accordance with grant requirements, the North Shore/Cape Ann Tobacco Alcohol Policy
Program accomplished the required program assessments for the Town of Marblehead. No
violations were identified in 2024.

In addition to the compliance checks, the North Shore/Cape Ann Tobacco Alcohol Policy
Program conducted one (1) full round of retailer inspections at each permitted
establishment as well as a pricing survey to access pricing trends across the state.

2. NORTH SHORE-CAPE ANN EMERGENCY PREPAREDNESS
COALITION
The North Shore-Cape Ann Emergency Preparedness Coalition works to prepare its
communities to respond to public health threats and emergencies, which include
terrorism and outbreaks of infectious diseases as well as emergencies caused by a
force of nature. This coalition, established in 2004, is a sub-coalition within region 3
of the Massachusetts Department of Public Health (MDPH) regional Public
Emergency Preparedness Coalitions and meets monthly. The coalition receives
limited funding from a grant provided by the Center of Disease Control (CDC) and is
mandated to attain certain standards in emergency preparedness. The North Shore-
Cape Ann Emergency Preparedness Coalition has established a website with
information for the public and links to other sites that can provide valuable
information to our residents. To date, the Board of Health has provided MDPH with
24/7 contact information, submitted a Continuity of Operations Plan, participated in
numerous drills, trainings, and conferences, trained key personnel in NIMS (National
Incident Management System) and ICS (Incident Control System), updated the All-
Hazards Plan, and has submitted a template of our Special Populations Plan.

The coalition is continuing the process of increasing its database of medical and non-
medical volunteers through our website and is offering training opportunities for these
volunteers. In the event of an infectious disease epidemic, pandemic, or a natural
disaster, volunteers will be greatly needed. Volunteers can register online and/or by
calling the Marblehead Board of Health.

3. CODE RED EMERGENCY NOTIFICATION SYSTEM
News and notifications concerning public health, trash and recycling are posted on
the Town’s website in a timely fashion. This useful media tool allows the posting of
links to sites relating to public health, emergency planning, solid waste, and beach
water quality. A subscription service on Marblehead.org allows people to sign up for

BUILDING COMMISSIONER & INSPECTIONAL SERVICES

MISSION:

The Building Department ensures public safety, health and welfare by regulating
construction through Federal, State and Local Codes and By-Laws. The Building
Department, in conjunction with the Fire Prevention Office, conducts annual
inspections of public and private schools, churches, temples, day care centers, food
service establishments, nursing homes, private clubs and places of assembly.

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS:

To the Citizens of Marblehead and the Select Board, the Building Inspectional
Services Department respectfully submits our 2024 Annual Report.

enforcement of life and building safety.

                       CEMETERY DEPARTMENT
The Marblehead Cemetery Department respectfully submits their Annual Report for
the Calendar year ending December 31, 2024.

Following Chapter 114 of the Mass General Laws, the Cemetery Department has
jurisdiction over all Town owned cemeteries.

The Cemetery Department is administered by a Superintendent, who is appointed by
a Cemetery Board of Commissioners. The elected three-member Board serves three-
year staggered terms and meets monthly to set policy, establish regulations and
oversee the cemetery budget.

A set of Rules and Regulations was adopted in 1945 to protect and promote the
general welfare of the Cemetery as a whole. These rules are updated periodically as
needed. All changes are carefully thought through and voted on by the Board.
Current Rules and Regulations are posted on www.Marblehead.org under Cemetery
Department.

This Department’s top priority is the delicate task of burying the deceased in a caring
and timely manner with as little disruption and discomfort as possible for families
and friends of the deceased. The department is also responsible for the complete care
and maintenance of Waterside and Harborview Cemeteries along with developing
available land for new grave space and reclaiming open land for cemetery use.

Within Waterside Cemetery, our full-time office clerk works diligently arranging
services, answering all questions related to Rules and Regulations, memorials,
genealogy, lot sales, lot location and all other general questions. The clerk is overseen
by the Superintendent assisting in the day-to-day operations of a very busy office.

Four full-time employees are responsible for all burials that occur, as well as the
complete maintenance of approximately forty acres within Waterside Cemetery and
Harborview Cemetery. This maintenance includes cutting grass. Trimming all
hedges, trimming back all trees, trimming around each upright memorial and flush
marker along with Spring and Fall cleanup of all leaves, flowers, baskets and wreaths
on lots. This small crew is also responsible for the maintenance of all buildings,
equipment, roads, water system, walls, fences, gates and benches within our Town
cemeteries. Cemetery personnel also install all cement foundations for upright
memorials and markers as well as repair stones where an expert is not required.

Cemetery vehicles and personnel also assist the Department of Public Works plowing
the streets of Marblehead during winter months along with other Town Departments.



                                          132


                                        133
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________


The maintenance staff for the Recreation and Parks Department is responsible for the
care and maintenance of our historical cemeteries, Old Burial Hill, Hooper Tomb,
Harris Street and Green Street Cemeteries.

There are no dogs allowed in our Town cemeteries. Signs are posted at most
entrances. Dog bylaws and ordinances apply and are in effect within the cemeteries
and will be enforced.

§ 13-11. Seasonal prohibition of dogs from certain areas. [Added 5-4-1992 ATM by
Art. 51; amended 5-1-2006 ATM by Art. 39; 5-7-2007 ATM by Art. 31]
No person owning, harboring or having custody or control of a dog shall permit
such dog to be on any public cemetery of the Town at any time throughout the
year, nor shall such person permit such dog to be on any public beach, athletic area,
playground or park of the Town between May 1 and October 1 of each year, provided,
however, that this prohibition shall not apply to Chandler Hovey Park, Crocker Park,
Fort Sewall, Fountain Park, Upper Seaside Park, Riverhead Beach or the Town
designated Dog Park. Whoever violates the provisions of this section shall pay a fine
in accordance with the following schedule:
         A. First offense in calendar year: $25.
          B. Second or subsequent offense within calendar year: $50

Due to its deteriorating state, currently the hourglass pool is filled and a flame lit for
the Memorial Day services only. The pool is shut down shortly following Memorial
Day. We do accept donations to the Eternal Flame Fund which has generated
$16,651.39 to date. We hope to raise enough to replace or repurpose the hourglass
pool.

The Cemetery Department’s operating budget comes from Annual Town Meeting
appropriations from taxation, department fees, and Perpetual Care Trust Fund
interest.

The Cemetery Sale of Lots Fund is a non-interest-bearing fund which we rely on for
the development of future grave space and much needed repairs to all buildings and
roads. The purchase of all major equipment is also paid out of this fund which is
generated by the actual sale of cemetery lots only. The Fund’s use is authorized by
the Cemetery Board of Commissioners and subject to Town Meeting appropriation.

                    CONSERVATION COMMISSION
This report is for Calendar Year 2024. The purpose of the Marblehead Conservation
Commission is twofold. First, to implement the safeguards specified in the state
Wetlands Protection Act, the state Stormwater Management Policy and the
Marblehead Wetlands Protection Bylaw in order to help preserve and protect the
wetlands and water resources in our Town. Second, to maintain and protect the
conservation lands held in trust for all residents of our Town. Of the six communities
adjacent to Salem Sound, Marblehead and Salem have the highest density of
population, with corresponding pressures on our open space and water resources. The
quality of life in Marblehead and its attractiveness is in part dependent on these
resources and we urge all Marblehead residents to join in our efforts to preserve and
protect them.

The Commission held regular meetings and public hearings throughout the year 2024.
We continued to assist residents in their construction projects while guiding them in
their endeavors to protect the wetland resources potentially affected by their
construction. During 2024, the Commission received and processed the following:

Notices of Intent                    28
Certificates of Compliance           64
Orders of Conditions                 17
Requests for Determinations          0
Minor Activity Permits               28
Enforcement Orders                   6
Emergency Certificates               0
Extension requests                   26
Minor Modification requests          2

A major ongoing effort of the Commission is the management and mitigation of
pollution caused by storm water runoff. This polluting runoff comes from streets,
parking lots, driveways and lawns. Lawn chemicals, oil and coolant leaks from
vehicles, animal waste, paints disposed of in catch basins (storms drains in our streets)
- all these pollutants/nutrients eventually end up in our streams and ocean. The
Marblehead Water & Sewer Department already follow the guidelines established by
the state to mitigate stormwater pollution runoff into our ocean waters. However, the
effort to mitigate this pollution will require a long-term change in the thinking and
actions on the part of the general public and in our approach to public infrastructure,
including the Historical Commission approving.

The Commission reminds everyone that our ponds are all in the process of filling in
with vegetation. This is a natural process, especially with shallow ponds, and is



                                           136


                                          137
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

caused or accelerated, in part, by the inflow of chemicals and fertilizers from abutting
lawns and storm water runoff. If you live directly on one of our ponds, please do not
fertilize your lawn.

Marblehead has exhausted (read developed) more than 95% of its buildable land.
With all the roofs, driveways, streets, parking areas and other impervious surfaces we
have built ourselves into one huge catch basin. This has exacerbated our drains
problems. All those little open fields, land depressions and pocket wetlands where
storm water used to be able to collect and gently seep into the ground are, for the most
part, gone. The Commission passed tree replacement regulations in 2023 to encourage
planting of additional tree canopy and to discourage removal of healthy trees. The
Commission recommends that other departments and the Select Board follow suit to
promote a healthy tree canopy in our Town.

We would like to remind everyone that the Conservation Commission currently has
no budget or staff for managing the conservation lands, currently over 133 acres.
Again this year, we wish to express our gratitude to the Marblehead Conservancy for
its continuing efforts to restore, improve and expand walking paths in our
conservation lands and for its ongoing program to remove invasive plant species,
which are strangling native vegetation in the conservation lands. The ongoing efforts
of the Conservancy to preserve and protect these very valuable resources are crucial
to the health of these areas, but the Commission will need to start requesting funding
to better maintain these important resources available for every resident and visitor
to enjoy. The Commission encourages you to explore the many walking trails in
Marblehead maintained by the Conservancy. Trail maps are available on the
Conservancy's web site.

Please visit us on the Town web site, www.marblehead.org, to find information on
our meeting schedules, help with filing permit applications and accessing the minutes
of our meetings.

Respectfully submitted, Marblehead Conservation Commission
David VanHoven, Chair
David Oster, Member
Kate Melanson, Member
Carole McCauley, Member
Kristopher Meola, Member
Jessica Norton, Member

                           COUNCIL ON AGING
The Council on Aging (COA) respectfully submits its annual report for the year
ending December 31, 2024.

The COA’s Mission: To empower older adults to age well, engage in the community,
and enrich their lives. Vision: Redefine Aging. Values: Service to the Community,
Respect for all factions of the Community, and continued growth and change as
needed.

Whereas the Town of Marblehead (MHD) funds two-thirds of the COA's salary
budget, the COA relies heavily on several outside sources to provide additional
funding necessary to support the remaining one-third of salaries and 90% of COA’s
operating expenses. Funding sources include the Marblehead Female Humane
Society (MFHS), the Friends of the Council on Aging (Friends), MassDOT grants,
the Older Americans Act of 1965 Titles IIIB & IIIC grants and Marblehead’s Shattuck
Memorial Fund, as well as private donations.

The Friends provided more than $40,000 in direct funding to the COA for expenses.
In November 2023, the Friends voted to provide additional funding towards the COA
Café' project. Initial estimates for this project were $150k and the Café was approved
by the MHD Select Board on December 9, 2023. In August 2024, the Café was placed
on hold due to the exceptionally high bids received. This project will be evaluated in
2026.

The Female Humane Society provided $35,000 in direct support of the COA’s
Transportation program. Funding is utilized for part-time drivers’ salaries. The COA
offers MHD residents age 60+ and disabled residents’ affordable access to
transportation for out-of-town medical appointments and groceries, and in-town for
medical appointments, groceries, MHD food pantry, the Library, the YMCA, JCC
and MHD Counseling Center, as well as errands to the post office/bank, Housing
department and to the Judy & Gene Jacobi Community Center for fitness/recreational,
educational, programming and lunch.

For FY24/25, the COA received a one-time $45,000 MassDOT ARPA for the
transportation program. The Shattuck Memorial Fund generously awarded both the
COA and Recreations and Parks department $50,000/ea., for an extreme makeover of
the back bocce court area of the Judy & Gene Jacobi Community Center. The bidding
process began in December 2024 with an estimated completion date of July 2025.

FY24 funding from the Executive Office of Elder Affairs (EOEA), the State Formula
Grant remains $15/per MHD resident age 60+. The Older Americans Act of 1965



                                        138


                                       139
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Title IIIB granted $10,000 and Title IIIC granted $14,000. Amounts fluctuate
annually and are utilized for salaries and services. It is important to note that more
than 1/3 of Marblehead’s residents are aged 60+.

Community Education/Safety: Safety Education and Community Awareness
continues to play pivotal roles at the COA. During 2024, 732 people attended a
variety of talks and discussions ranging from “Safety Tips with Chief King”, weekly
Coffee with a Cop, an informative afternoon with the League of Women Voters, a
Medicare Annual Open Enrollment presentation, and an interactive afternoon with
Patricia Sullivan, DPT about her recently published book “30 Years of Teaching,
Treating and Travel.” Pam Petersen, from the Marblehead Historical Society
presented “Where the Bodies Are Buried” to a group of 40 people and several citizens
attended an informational dialogue regarding the upcoming Town Meeting Warrant
Articles. Our nine-week Citizens Police Academy held in February hosted 13
attendees. IPHONE/IPAD instructional classes were offered throughout the year with
class size of 6 students per session receiving detailed tutoring in use of their devices.

Fitness/Exercise: 16,285 Fitness classes were held with a total of 627 participants
availing themselves of a variety of exercise programing. Our Fitness classes provide
something for everyone and include a wide variety of programs such as Step-it-Up,
Muscle Conditioning, Strong Bones/Strong Bodies, Osteo Prevention, Balance &
Mobility, Strength & Stretch, Weight Training, Line Dancing, Yoga, Chair Yoga and
Zumba Gold. Chair Pilates and Pilates became new additions to the Fitness calendar
in 2024. The COA also has a Fitness center generously provided by the Shattuck
Memorial Fund and many participants enjoy use of the equipment.

Health/Wellness Services: Our Footcare program, Senior Footwise, which offers
specialized footcare with a registered nurse continues to grow. Services are now
offered bi-monthly. Bi-weekly Parkinsons Fitness classes, generously sponsored thru
the Parkinson Foundation, range in class size from 7 to 17 attendees. Classes are free
for all participants. The monthly Parkinsons support group, Choose Happy, provides
a forum to share experiences and thoughts in a welcoming, supportive environment.

Weekly Board of Health (BOH) blood pressure clinics are offered at the COA with
anywhere from 8 to 15 seniors who meet with a nurse. 100 residents attended the
Board of Health annual high dose Flu Clinic.

Health Insurance Counseling: The SHINE program (Serving the Health Insurance
Needs of Everyone) is a federally funded program, and volunteers are trained in
Medicare health insurance as well as prescription coverage. SHINE counselors Peter
Bowen, Lu-Ann Gabel, Kelly Upham and Deb Ventresca met with a total of 640
seniors in 2024 or a 14% increase compared to 2023. 2024 age demographics: (12%)



                                          139


                                             140
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

were age 64 or younger, (50%) or 323 participants were age 65-74, (32%) age 75-84
percentage and (6%) age 85+ percentage. Appointments are available throughout the
year.

Intergenerational: Wyatt Foley completed his interactive “Discover Movie Magic”
program in March 2024. For 2025, three Juniors, Phineas Jakious, Quinn Scheib and
Matias Watts Cruz kicked off their season with the showing of “It’s a Wonderful
Life”. Ten COA seniors attend bi-monthly events with the Film Club members
students at Marblehead High School.

The High school students who initiated a Scrapbooking Club have continued to
participate in activities here alongside our seniors through the Fall of 2024 and into
2025. Competition thrives in chair volleyball or curling matches between the two
groups. In October, more than twenty ninth grade students visited the COA on
Volunteer Day and assisted the Friends COA with their annual fundraising campaign.
They stuffed and labeled over 6,000 envelopes.

The Marblehead Garden Club along with Tower School students made and decorated
Holiday ornaments that accompanied Holiday Goodie bags. Glover School students
made special cards for Veterans and Mrs. McMahon’s & Mrs. Bruett’s third grade
Village School students made Valentines Day cards for our Tuesday lunch group.
The Driftwood Garden Club created beautiful wreaths and swags, and the
Swampscott Garden Club (50% Swampscott & 50% Marblehead members) prepared
beautiful holiday table arrangements.

Nutrition: 5,549 Grab-n-Go and Traveling Chef meals were provided to 263 seniors.
These programs are offered through Agespan. Grab-n-Go meals are provided three
days a week and the Traveling Chef program is a special monthly sit-down luncheon.
The COA’s Tuesday Lunch program provided 3,149 meals to 287 seniors and is
sponsored by the FCOA. The sit-down luncheon requires 10-12 dedicated volunteers
to assist COA staff with food prep, set-up/break down/serving/clean up. The
Marblehead Rotary Club generously donated $1,400 to provide a special lobster roll
lunch for 65 meals on wheels participants. Meals were delivered by members of the
COA Board, COA Friends Board, Staff, and Rotary members. The COA provides
weekly transportation to the MHD Food pantry, Market Basket & Crosbys.

Outreach: The COA’s Outreach Coordinator (OC) is a licensed social worker who
receives referrals from various Town Dept’s. Among them are the Assessor’s office,
Police dispatch, Fire, Housing, Counseling Center, Clerk, Veterans Office, and
Animal Control. In addition, the (OC) has worked collaboratively on cases with many
local medical offices as well as our regional Aging Service Access Point (ASAP),




                                        140


                                       141
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Agespan. The OC maintains alliance with the MHD Fire Dept Home Safety Program,
and 59 Home safety evaluations and lockbox installations occurred in 2024.

The OC responds to an ongoing number of inquiry phone calls weekly. These calls
may require information, referral, assistance with rental issues, food insecurities or
fuel assistance. Many calls can be resolved over the phone, yet several result in
office/home visits or ongoing case management. The topics often discussed are
referrals for Mental Health assistance, Dementia-related questions, Caregiver
Support, housing, homelessness, emergency assistance, emergency hospitalization,
Adult Day health and medical appointment transportation to Boston. The number of
monthly informational and consulting calls this year ranged from 52 to 142.

The Mission of the COA is to promote healthy aging. One of the many lessons we
learned from the pandemic is the profound impact loneliness has on our mental and
physical health. “Loneliness acts as a fertilizer for other disease”. (Dr. Steve Cole,
Ph.D., UCLA) The COA approaches social isolation from many perspectives and
offers a variety of recreation, nutrition, fitness, and creative arts programs. The OC
continues to collaborate with agencies and individuals within the community to
address social isolation. This generally involves getting a little bit ‘out’ of one’s
comfort zone. The OC continued with luncheon trips to Essex Tech’s Maple Street
Bistro which always attracts 10-20 seniors. The “Meetups” continued throughout
most of the year to include Sunday brunches, movies, Great House at Castle Hill,
museums, art classes with local artists in their studios using different mediums. A
total of 15 such events were planned with anywhere from 5 to 20 people attending.

The OC and the Adult Program Director of the Jewish Community Center (JCC), with
assistance from Marblehead Public Schools transportation, were able to bring approx.
30 seniors to the memorable Auschwitz exhibit in Boston. A Salem Ferry trip to
Boston has become a popular annual excursion and the numbers of participants are
rising yearly. Dan Dixey’s Documentary on local lobstermen and their families in
town brought out at least 50 seniors in town. Thanks to the generosity of the FCOA
the Salem Ferry trip and Dan Dixies documentary tickets were offered for both events
at discounted rates. Working collaboratively with other agencies to keep seniors “in
the loop” within our community is essential. These activities could take the form of
a speaker series, Dementia-specific training, health, home improvements,
multigenerational programs, health insurance and musical performances.

A sampling of some of the initiatives organized by the OC this year were:
•        Partnered with the office of Pat Lausier, Esq in Town to present on a
“Financial check-up” for seniors brought a crowd of over 30 people. The OC refers
frequently to the Family Caregiver Support group at Agespan. The COA and Agespan
hosted a Memory Café here which was fully attended, and we are hoping to create a



                                        141


                                           142
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

similar activity to help caregivers and their loved ones in 2025. The OC also partners
with the AARP’s Speaker series and a “Retirement Planning seminar” was very well
attended in May.
•        The OC has introduced other holistic health options to the community such
as Acupuncture. Autumn Acupuncture hosted a seminar here which drew over 20
seniors and is now offering group acupuncture at their office at reduced rates for
seniors.
•        The OC set up a presentation by Homeworks Mass Energy program offering
free Home Energy assessments and ways to save on your bills. Approximately 30
people attended.
•        The OC introduced the Mass Senior Action Council to the center through an
ice cream social. This agency is a force for advocating for seniors and the main topic
was the Medicare Savings program (MSP). In that week alone, 20 people were signed
up for this program. At the very least it would pay for their Medicare monthly
premiums.
•        MHD High School Choir performed during the Holidays at the Senior
Center.

Being present in the community is not only a reminder that we are “still here”, but
also that we seniors can, and do contribute, in a variety of ways

•        The OC and COA Director participated in the Sustainable Energy Fair this
Fall which drew over 150 participants including many seniors.
•        The OC and COA Program Coordinator participated in a Boutique Market
at the Corinthian Yacht Club. We connected with over 100 members throughout the
day and welcomed some new seniors into a variety of our programs.
•        Over 50 seniors and several COA employees attended the Veterans after
hours fundraisers at the VFW and the Beacon restaurant. COA employees and
members also participated in Wreaths Across America.

The OC continued running the Flying Solo and Caregiver Support Groups in 2024.
Members of the Flying Solo group have established friendships and now get together
outside of our group socially. Our Caregiver Support Group has a steady 7-12
attendees, and most attended the Dementia Caregiving presentation by Karen
Coppola, CDP that was offered in our speaker series. We continue to invite guest
speakers from MHD Police & Fire depts, Agespan and the medical community.

The OC continues to make home visits and schedule office visits with elders in the
community. A home visit remains a great assessment tool to see how the seniors are
managing in their environment. Office visits are for applying for various types of
assistance, referrals and emotional support. This year 511 home visits were recorded
and 353 office visits. The OC continues to help with either initial SNAP applications



                                        142


                                       143
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

or renewals/referral for new Fuel Assistance applications. The OC assisted with
approx. 45 applications this year. Once enrolled, the consumer does not have to return
to the OC the following year unless there is a problem. Both programs will be
impacted if the current administration freezes federal funding for our citizens.

•       The OC attended 44 meetings over the year which included Marblehead
Female Humane Society, Housing meetings, Mass COA conference seminars, Boston
Food Bank, MHD Police, MHD Fire, JCC, medical offices, Agespan, Greater Lynn
Senior Services (GLSS), and Hoarding Training.

•        We are blessed in our community to receive generous donations of Holiday
Goodie bags, wreaths, swags, and gift certificates donated by the Tower School
students, Marblehead Garden Club, Driftwood Garden Club, MHD & Swampscott
Garden Club, Crosby’s Market Place and Making Ends Meet. Our staff members,
including our drivers and the OC had the privilege of delivering these items to over
100 seniors in our community.

RECREATION: COA programming provides socialization, laughter, and in many
cases, some friendly competition. A total of 6,726 recreational events ran during 2024
with a total of 368 people taking part in those programs. Programs range from drop-
in-Bridge to Quilting with Janet and drop-in-Knitting. In December, the knitters held
their annual Holiday Boutique, where they sold their exquisite hand knit/crocheted
items and raised more than $3,000 for the COA. Thursday afternoon Canasta was
added to our recreational programming in 2024 and has rapidly become a popular
addition to our schedule, while Mahjong maintains between 12-16 players twice
weekly. Movie afternoons continued with several newly released movies shown
during 2024. They included Killers of the Flower Moon, Oppenheimer, & Thelma.
Earlier released films were comprised of titles like Megan Leavey and The Peanut
Butter Falcon.

Golf continues from May through September with Maureen Horan scheduling the
more than 25 players twice a week. Bocce, Chair Volleyball and Indoor Curling
programs continue to surpass previous years’ numbers, both in terms of players and
record number of games played. Members totaled 84, up from 75 the previous year.
The Bocce “travel team” hosted several tournaments throughout the season and
visited Beverly, Wenham and Peabody COA’s for a bit of affable rivalry.

Throughout the Bocce season, weekly matches were held with the Anchor to
Windward community, including a yearly tournament named the ATW Invitational
Tournament, where the ATW players invite the Bocce players.

                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Social/Celebration Events: Our Special Events programs were just one of the many
highlights of 2024 at the COA! Through the superb efforts of Teresa Collins, who
spearheaded making Marblehead a PURPLE HEART COMMUNITY, in August
the COA was honored to host a luncheon for our Purple Heart recipients and their
families. Our semi-annual Veterans Breakfasts took place at the COA both in May
and November with 74 Veterans enjoying and socializing over a delicious breakfast
prepared and paid for by the Philanthropic Lodge. These bi-annual events are held
to honor and pay tribute to our Veterans. A special day trip to the American Heritage
Museum was held in September with 28 Veterans attending the event. Funding for
both transportation and reduced tickets was provided by the National Grand Bank and
the Friends of the COA. Our twelfth annual Thanksgiving at the Masons was another
well-attended, much anticipated event. 131 seniors enjoyed a delicious full
Thanksgiving dinner prepared and paid for by Louise Moore and the Masons. St.
Patrick’s Day Special Luncheon was held at the Gerry V, with a traditional St.
Patrick’s meal served to 88 people. The Friends of the COA hosted their Holiday
Open House catered event by Every Little Breeze. More than 120 Guests enjoyed a
day of socializing and a delicious buffet style brunch. Perhaps one of the most
anticipated Special events was the July “Lobster Roll Luncheon,” complete with
chips and dessert. A private donor, the Friends of the COA and Patriots Seafood
made this event possible. 120 patrons enjoyed a delicious lobster roll prepared with
their choice of either butter or mayonnaise.

Transportation: More than 5,116 rides for 132 seniors were provided in 2024. 35%
of seniors were transported for nutrition services such as the MHD food pantry,
grocery stores and to the COA for lunch. Access to medical appts was provided to
38% of riders. The remaining 27% went to Abbot library and in-town for errands to
the bank, pharmacy, hairdressers, YMCA, JCC, Counseling Center, Housing and to
the COA for fitness, educational and recreational programming. Through a MassDOT
grant and $38,000 in funding by the Shattuck Memorial Fund, the COA replaced its
2009 and 2016 vans with a brand-new minivan and minibus.

Trips: Several opportunities for travel were presented this year, including trips to
Alaska, Sicily, Monaco and San Antonio. Local trips enjoyed lunch and entertainment
at Danversport Yacht Club, a trip into Boston for a Harbor Cruise with luncheon
buffet, Lake Winnipesaukee boat cruise with luncheon followed by a visit to Castle
in the Clouds and a Jimmy Buffet Tribute lobster luncheon at Fosters in York Maine.

Volunteer Opportunities: Approved at Town Meeting, the amount a Sr. Tax Work
Off volunteer can earn towards their real estate tax credit increased from $750 to
$2,000. This is the first increase in 20 years. Fifteen seniors participated in 2024 and
provided 1,461 volunteer hours to six Town depts. Twenty-one volunteers provided
1,043 hours of hands-on support for the “Tuesday lunch” program. Additionally, the



                                         144


                                        145
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

COA relies on volunteers for daily Fitness Center monitoring, handing out grab-n-go
meals, fitness instructors, the Friends of the COA to fundraise, COA Board members
and much, much more. More than 3,000 volunteer hours were recorded. A new
volunteer garden group was created over the summer months. This amazing group
cleaned up our outside gardens, infusing them with new life. This group of eight
worked tirelessly weeding, raking, planting and watering our gardens. This year, the
Annual Volunteer Luncheon was held at the Salem Hawthorne Hotel and sponsored
by Friends of the COA. More than 70 volunteers attended.

For FY25, the Town of Marblehead appropriated $406,699 to the COA for salaries
and expenses. This funding provided 60% of the total budget required to provide these
outstanding services.

It is also important to report current age statistics to Marblehead residents:

“The Commonwealth of Massachusetts considers anyone to be a senior who is 60
years of age or older (https://www.mass.gov/orgs/executive-office-of-aging-
independence). In 2024, the youngest of the baby boomers turned 60. “The number
of older Americans has increased by 34% (14.7 million) since 2012, compared to an
increase of 2% in the population under 65.” (2023 Profile of Older Americans
Publication 2024, Pg.4) “By 2030, all baby boomers will be over 65 years old. This
will cause the number of people of retirement age to increase to one in five
Americans”).(https://www.urban.org/policy-centers/cross-center-
initiatives/program-retirement-policy/projects/data-warehouse/what-future-holds/us-
population-aging)

“Councils on Aging and Senior Centers are municipal agencies that oversee the
programming, services, and centers that support older adults in their community.”
(https://www.mass.gov/info-details/aging-services-network)    The      Town    of
Marblehead is comprised of more than 6,200 (31%) residents aged 60+ and the
Town’s         median        age      of     residents     is     49.6      years.
(https://datausa.io/profile/geo/marblehead-ma).

The Judy & Gene Jacobi Community Center (Jacobi Center) was built twenty-seven
years ago and is where the COA is located. The COA has access to the Jacobi Center
40 hours a week, or 38% of all available hours.

In addition, the Jacobi Center has had extensive water leaks throughout the building
for more than a decade. Tiles have been replaced and continue to be replaced. The
air conditioning and heating system(s) throughout the building are barely functional
and the bathrooms are in desperate need of a major upgrade.




                                         145


                                             146
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Given that the COA only has one building in which to operate its programs, combined
with the rapidly growing number of people over age 60, it is imperative for Town
Officials and residents to evaluate both the building for safety and functionality as
well as to review and determine whether this building should remain the home of the
COA. The COA needs its own building space at days/times that will accommodate
our growing senior population. This concern has been presented to the Town’s
Charter Committee, and we are hopeful that discussions will occur in 2025.

Respectfully submitted: Lisa J. Hooper,
MS - Director

                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS

The mission of the Town of Marblehead Department of Public Works (DPW) is to
maintain and improve the Town’s public ways, stormwater infrastructure, public
shade trees, vehicular fleet and to coordinate public way snow and storm operations
in a safe, cost effective, and environmentally sustainable manner.

                           DIVISION REPORTS

HIGHWAY DIVISION

The Highway Division maintains nearly 70 miles of public streets and sidewalks in
Town, including but not limited to, repairing trenches and patching potholes,
sidewalk maintenance and rehabilitation, street sweeping, storm and accident debris
removal, maintenance and repair of Town fences and street signs, pavement marking,
roadside landscaping, and Christmas tree removal. The Division also has oversight of
all Public way projects and supplies equipment or labor as required for other Town
Department’s tasks.

STORM WATER (DRAIN) DIVISION

The storm water system includes over 4,000 catch basins and manholes. The
Storm Water Division’s crew responds to flooding issues, blocked storm lines,
resident inquiries, the repair of manholes and catch basins, the cleaning and
maintenance of stormwater lines and the re-stablishing of drainage ditches. The
Division also has oversight of all Storm Water Projects

TREE DIVISION

Public shade trees located on public ways are under the jurisdiction of the Tree
Warden and are guided by the Massachusetts General Bylaws Chapter 87 and the
Town of Marblehead Tree Bylaws. The Tree Warden duties include inspection of all
tree inquiries, whether made by phone, email, or through the online reporting system
at marblehead.org. The tree crew is responsible for day-to-day operations including
tree removal, pruning, planting and stump removal. This year, the lack of rain from
early summer through the growing season into late in the fall was a source of stress
for Town shade trees. The Towns pear and cherry trees recovered from last year’s
extreme artic cold snap and pushed out flowers this spring after little or no flowering
last year.

The Tree Division purchased 100 seedlings from the Massachusetts Tree Wardens
Association that were planted and cared for in conservation areas by the Marblehead
Conservancy and other volunteers.

The Tree Division would like to thank residents that made donations to the Town’s
tree planting fund. Money from the fund went directly to tree purchasing and planting
this year.

Set Back Tree Planting
At Town meeting residents approved Article 42 Public Shade Trees; Protection:
to amend the General Bylaws, Chapter 177 Article IV to add a new Section-9, Set
Back Tree Planting. The Town now may plant trees within 20 feet of the public
right of way, provided that written permission from the adjoining property
owner is obtained first consistent with Massachusetts G.L. c 87, sec. 7. The
Article will aid in creating optional tree planting locations to help maintain tree
canopy as the Town works to upgrade the sidewalks to ADA compliance.

Arbor Day
In the Spring of 2024 Fort Sewall was the site for Marblehead’s Arbor Day project.
Mayer Tree donated a day of service pruning trees inside the park as part of the
ongoing Fort Sewall restoration. For the fourth year, the Tree Warden worked with
Sustainable Marblehead and local volunteers to identify and plant trees in
neighborhoods that had little or no street trees to help reduce the “heat island” in these
areas. This year, the median island on Brookhouse Drive was chosen as the planting
site. On a Saturday in the spring, over 20 bare root trees were planted with the help
of volunteers.




                                 Arbor Day 2024

FLEET MECHANICS DIVISION

The Mechanics Division of DPW maintains and completes maintenance of the
Town’s vehicular fleet. The Division completes regular preventive maintenance and
emergency repairs. The Division operates during all storm events and emergency
situations to minimize any delay in completing critical work.

                    SNOW AND STORM OPERATIONS

The DPW is the Town’s lead Department for all winter storm operations. The Town
of Marblehead winter storm response is conducted by Town staff only. Departments
that comprise the Snow Team are: DPW (all Divisions), Water and Sewer
Departments, Recreation and Parks Department, Cemetery Department, Health
Department, and School Department.

The winter of 2024 had approximately 13.3 inches of snow and no blizzard
categorized storms. Ice control was part of every winter weather event. The increase
demand for road treatment and cost of Salt were the largest portion of the Snow
budget in 2024. The Snow Team is still adjusting to the removal of the historic winter
parking ban. To provide the same level of service, the DPW has noted increased hours
and difficulty meeting the same snow pushback limits.

When a snow event of any size is forecasted, the Department requests all residents
find off street parking to support snow fighting efforts, regardless of snow restrictions
being issued. This allows the Department to conduct efficient snow clearing efforts
and minimizes the chances of damage. The Department encountered difficulty
maintaining the same level of service even after applying additional time and
resources due to the lack of the traditional winter parking ban.

We encourage you to review the Snow Emergency information at Marblehead.org for
helpful tips and answers to frequently asked questions regarding snow situations.

                            Message from The Director

The Department of Public Works continues with the development of a Master
Transportation Plan. This plan will include the coordination of infrastructure
improvements that impact the schedules for final paving and sidewalk upgrades. The
DPW met quarterly with the Water and Sewer Commission and National Grid to
review the utility’s capital improvement plans and coordinate the sequencing of
projects, working in conjunction with the Town’s pavement management plan.

2024 highlighted the reorganization of staffing to create a structure that encourages
employee development and sustainability. This included a review and revision of
existing job descriptions in the Highway, Tree, and Storm Water Division. The
addition of three new positions Working Foreman Construction, Working Foreman
Maintenance, and Specialized Heavy Equipment Operator, were included in this
process. Jobs were posted, interviews conducted, and I am happy to say all
promotions were current DPW employees. This new structure allows employees to
be cross trained in the responsibilities of the multiple divisions, giving the DPW the
fluidity needed to meet the demands of the residents, keeping within the approved
budget and maintaining the same employee count.

At the creation of the Community Development and Planning Department at the 2024
Annual Town Meeting, the responsibilities of the Conservation Agent were
incorporated into the Town Planner’s duties. This allowed for a reconfiguration of the
Town Engineer’s duties and the moving of the Town Engineer’s position to the
Department of Public Works. In 2024, Maggie Wheeler was appointed Town
Engineer. All at DPW are pleased to welcome Maggie Wheeler and are enthusiastic
about the improved collaboration of the Town’s resources.




                                        157


                                           158
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Since the general override did not pass at the 2024 Annual Town Meeting, a onetime
use of the Street Opening Revolving funds was diverted for FY25 to support section
116, Rebuild/Maintain/ Hot Top Pavement/Streets, budget line items. In FY26 the
revolving funds will return to historic use and support the trench repair program.

I would like to thank all the Tower Way employees who worked tirelessly providing
a high level of service throughout the year in the absence of a ratified MMEU
Contract. All employees contributed to the successful completion of the DPW
mission in 2024 and provided excellent service while maintaining the Town’s Public
Ways, Public Shade Trees, Town Fleet and Storm Water System.


Amy McHugh
Director of Public Works

                          FINANCIAL SERVICES
The Financial Services department is responsible for the Town’s financial functions,
including accounting, payroll, revenue collection, technology, budgeting, capital
financing and retirement management. The Financial Services department is
overseen by the Town's Finance Director. The Finance Director also serves as the
Chief Financial Officer and is responsible for the management of information
technology for the Town. In addition, the Finance Director/CFO serves as an ex-
officio member of the Town’s Retirement Board and the Town’s Compensation
Committee.

Within Financial Services, the Accounting office is responsible for the audit and
approval of all town expenditures and the preparation of various accounting and
financial reports required by the State’s Department of Revenue.                   The
Treasurer/Collector's office is responsible for the distribution of payroll and vendor
checks and the collection of taxes and fees owed to the Town. The Payroll office
performs all payroll functions and administers various employee benefits, including
health insurance, dental insurance, flexible spending plans, 457 deferred
compensation plans, and life insurance. The Payroll office also manages
unemployment and workers' compensation benefits.

The Financial Services Department publishes a separate Annual Comprehensive
Financial Report (ACFR) for the Town on a fiscal year basis (July 1 to June 30) each
year. A copy of the latest ACFR (FY2023) is available on the Town’s website or by
contacting the Finance Department.

Following are the more significant accomplishments of the Financial Services
department during 2024:

    •    Working on the Certificate of Achievement for Excellence in Financial
         Reporting from the Government Finance Officers Association for the
         seventeenth year. This award is only given to government units whose
         annual comprehensive financial reports (ACFRs) achieve the highest
         standards in government accounting and financial reporting.

    •    Working closely with a new independent CPA audit firm to prepare and
         issue the Town’s FY2024 ACFR.

    •    In the context of financing, the Town has maintained its AAA bond rating
         by the independent rating agency Standard & Poor’s (S&P). Marblehead is
         one of 69 Massachusetts communities to receive the highest municipal
         rating.



                                        163


                                           164
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________


    •   Completed the annual open enrollment process in April and May for
        employee and retiree health insurance. The Town began purchasing health
        insurance plans through the State’s Group Insurance Commission (GIC) in
        July 2012 and is currently in a six-year agreement that is effective through
        FY2026.

    •   Conducted an open enrollment in May for optional dental insurance
        programs for employees and retirees. Under this program, employees and
        retirees pay 100% of the premiums for this insurance through a payroll
        deduction.

    •   Working on Tyler Munis ERP system conversion for the General Ledger.

    •   The Financial Forecast presented and completed the Tax Recap December.

    •   Received grant funding through our membership with the NorthShore IT
        Collaborative for MFA and a new .gov domain. The Collaborative provides
        enhanced security, greater service, and additional grant opportunities.

    •   Working on the Department of Revenue reports within specified deadlines,
        including the balance sheet for Free Cash, and other accounting forms.
