                             PLANNING BOARD

The Planning Board is an elected board comprised of five members each with a three-
year staggered terms and one appointed associate member. The board members for
the year 2024 were Robert Schaeffner, Barton Hyte, Edward Nilsson, and Andrew
Christensen, and Marc Liebman. Steven Leverone served as the board associate
member. The Town Planner, Alex Eitler, oversees the board's administrative process
and provides technical assistance to prospective applicants and interested parties.

The planning board meets regularly on the second Tuesday of each month and as
needed at other times. The regular meetings were held remotely on zoom for the first
half of the year and began meeting in person in the Select Board’s room during the
Fall. The agendas are posted on the Town’s website and at the office of the Town
Clerk.

It is the responsibility of the planning board to approve different types of development
including divisions of land to create new lots, all new nonresidential buildings over
700 square feet and all new residential construction and also additions that are over
500 square feet within the shoreline and harbor front districts. In addition to the site
plan approval special permits and subdivision control approvals, the board issues
smart growth special permits, wireless communication special permits, incentive
zoning special permits and land disturbance permits for larger projects when there is
no conservation commission jurisdiction. State and local laws which govern the
process for these approvals are Massachusetts General Laws (MGL) Chapter 40A
(the Zoning Act); MGL Chapter 41, Sections 81K – 81GG (the Subdivision Control
Law); the Town of Marblehead Subdivision Rules and Regulations Chapter 258 of
the by-laws of the town of Marblehead, MGL Chapter 40A and the Town of
Marblehead Zoning Bylaws.

Permits issued in 2024 include 1 Approval Not Required – Form A applications
(ANR) and nine 9 Site Plan Approval Special Permits

At the 2024 Annual Town Meeting, the Planning Board sponsored an article to
establish a new zoning district that was compatible with the MBTA 3A state
requirements. The adoption of the proposed zoning district failed. The Planning
Board assisted with the research and development of this proposed zoning district and
provided key advice on how to best tackle this state requirement.

The Planning Board thanks the Building Department for information on zoning
interpretation and other assistance.




                                         214


                                        217
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

The Board thanks Maggie Wheeler, the Town Engineer, who provides technical
assistance to the board and to prospective applicants. Planning Board applications
are processed in the Engineering Department. The board extends special thanks to
Lisa Lyons and Jenn Smith for their assistance in keeping the board running
smoothly.

We offer special thanks and congratulations to Rebecca Curran Cutting, Marblehead
Town Planner, for her many years of administrative support to the Planning Board
and her contributions and tireless efforts ensuring that the board and Marblehead
residents were served well. Her knowledge, expertise, dedication and attention to
detail will be greatly missed. We extend our gratitude and wish her well as she
embarks on her next adventure!

Robert Schaeffner, Chairman

                           POLICE DEPARTMENT
The Marblehead Police Department (MPD) is pleased to submit to the citizens of
Marblehead our Annual Report for the year ending December 31, 2024.

The accomplishments highlighted in this report are the direct result of the
commitment to excellence by sworn personnel, our Joint Dispatch Center, and MPD’s
civilian support staff.

MISSION:
The MPD is committed to the protection of lives and property, and the safety of the
community we serve, both residents and visitors. We strive to maintain the highest
standard of professional and proven police practices and to preserve individual rights
and liberties afforded to all citizens under the law. We are dedicated to a procedural,
transparent and cooperative effort with the community to identify and address their
concerns, reduce the fear of, and actual, crime, participate in criminal justice and
social service diversion programs and improve the quality of life of our residents.

OPERATIONAL OVERVIEW:
For the year 2024, MPD handled 22,888 calls for service, an 17% increase over 2023.
Much of this increase included officer initiated proactive patrols, selective traffic
enforcement and community engagement. As each member of the department was
asked to take on these increases, it was a team effort to produce the culminative
results. Our highest number of calls includes - medical calls (1595); burglar alarms
(456); parking complaints (745); motor vehicle stops (834); motor vehicle accidents
(299) and Property Checks (5,583). The E-911 Dispatch center handled more than
150,000 calls, and serves as the Town communication hub for fire, ambulance, and
police emergency response, while also servicing all walk-in activity to the police
station. An “all hands-on deck” approach is the MPD way. Officers, Supervisors,
Dispatchers, and our civilian staff take on a variety of duties, to provide police service
effectively and efficiently to the Town. The Police Department focused considerable
efforts and resources on activities such as major events; community engagement;
technology improvements; restorative justice work; specialized training; policy
review and development; employee health and wellness; data analysis; grant writing;
and operational functions.

Accreditation: The Massachusetts Police Accreditation Program is based upon
national standards established by the Commission on Accreditation for Law
Enforcement Agencies (CALEA). These standards reflect the best professional
practices in police management, administration, operations, and support services. Our
commitment will ensure that MPD will continue to deliver professional police




                                          216


                                         219
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

service, with many added benefits to the Town, such as reducing liability, and lower
insurance rates.

Court actions and Crime trends: In 2024, the Marblehead Police Department
(MPD) had (46) forty-six arrests and (5) five protective custodies. We submitted
(103) one hundred and three criminal applications which resulted in (16) sixteen
probable cause hearings before Magistrates at Lynn District Court. We applied for
(11) arrest warrants and (19) nineteen criminal complaint summonses.

Our overall crime rate in Marblehead went down 7% in 2024. Property crime is down
9%, while crimes against the person are up 23%. MPD continues to focus on
educating the public regarding active fraudulent schemes, crime sprees (car breaks),
while using technology and proactive patrols to reduce vandalism and other crimes
throughout Town. The Municipal Road safety grant, used for directed impaired
driving patrols, aims to keep drunk driving offenses down (which it is). Victim follow
ups, and collaboration with HAWC and a strong local community advocacy, has
provided services to victims of domestic and intimate violence crimes. MPD
Detectives and Patrol officers continue to conduct thorough initial and follow up
investigations, while proactively patrolling neighborhoods to reduce theft, vandalism
or other illicit activity.

                                Crimes      Crimes     Crimes     Group      Total
                                Against     Against    Against    B
                                Property    Society    Persons    Crimes

 2023                           271         6          77         187        541
 2024                           246         10         95         152        503
 % change                       - 9%        +67%       +23%       -19%       -7%

JDP Program and Essex County Outreach
During 2024, our Jail Diversion Mental Health Clinician Gina Rabbitt (who became
a State licensed Mental Health Clinician in 24’) was responsible for (241) two
hundred and forty-one cases involving individuals suffering from either a mental
health or substance abuse related issue. One hundred and ninety-three (193) of the
cases were diverted from the Emergency Department (ED). Two hundred and twenty-
five (225) of the cases were diverted from the Justice System. MPD also partnered
with Essex County Outreach to further divert individuals from the criminal justice
system, while finding them resources and treatment for substance use disorders.
These follow-ups with ECCO have become regular and a life changer for many, AND
they are diverting people from the Justice System. MPD has also partnered with
Police Assisted Addiction and Recovery (PARI) to identify resources for officers to
utilize when needed and expand naloxone and fentanyl strip distribution.



                                        217


                                           220
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Traffic/Parking Enforcement: In 2024, MPD investigated (305) three hundred and
five hundred traffic accidents, conducted eight hundred and thirty-four (834) motor
vehicle stops and issued (572) five hundred and seventy-two citations for traffic
violations. We had (13) thirteen arrests and (48) forty-eight criminal applications
involving Chapter 90 violations. Out of the above citations, (19) nineteen resulted in
civil motor vehicle hearings before Magistrates at LDC. MPD responded to seven
hundred and forty-five (745) parking complaints, issued fifteen hundred and forty-
three (1543) parking tickets with $49,200 in fines.

To help identify our problem traffic locations, we conducted nineteen (19) traffic
studies, which resulted in four hundred and fifty-one (451) selective traffic
enforcement patrols, to educate and enforce speed limits and other traffic violations.
MPD has also worked with the Traffic Safety Advisory Committee to address the
traffic change requests that have been received, as well as help facilitate the
improvement of traffic safety in Town. Several neighborhood traffic concerns were
discussed through this forum, identifying and clarifying needs, while providing Town
residents with options and data, needed to help make real time decisions and for
planning in the future.

Community Services and Engagement: MPD is proud of every one of our efforts
to connect with the community. In 2024, we added and continued several programs,
while continuing our unique collaboration with the Council on Aging through the
Citizen Police Academy, TRIAD, Coffee with a Cop, High School community
service day, and Faith and Blue events at Temple Emanu-El and the Clifton Lutheran
Church, Car Seat Install Day held at the MPD and the Sustainability Fair at the Brown
School. We held one Citizen Police Academy, while starting the planning for a follow
up “master” CPA program. Janice Salisbury-Beal leads our TRIAD team (TRIAD is
a collaborative effort among local police, Fire, Council on Aging, Sheriff’s
department and interested individual seniors) and its monthly meetings. MPD
personnel attended regular community meetings (88 attended by patrol – dozens by
the Detectives, SRO and administrative staff) with a diverse cross section of civic
groups and neighborhood associations, to include both Rotary organizations, cub
scouts, boy scouts, girl scouts, the LOWV, Fairwinds Child Day Care Center, the
Marblehead Racial Justice Team, School PCOs, TFAD, MMHTF, the ADL’s North
Shore advisory board, and The ADL Law Enforcement committee. MPD ran another
successful Junior Criminal Investigator class with the Recreation and Park
Department and met with hundreds of students in Town through special visits to
schools, and tours of the police station.

Department staff participated in several civic engagements; to include Thanksgiving
Dinner organized by the COA at the Masonic Temple; the Breast Friends Cancer
Walk, Special Olympic Polar Plunges, Cruiser Convoy, and Law Enforcement Torch



                                        218


                                       221
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

runs, attending the Negro Election Day Parade in Salem, Essex County District
Attorney Summer Camp, the Jimmy Fund MA Chiefs Boston Marathon campaign,
and the Doug Flutie Foundation for autism awareness. In solidarity with our Jewish
and other Faith Based communities, MPD attended several events and trainings to
address the rise in antisemitism and hate and hosted active threat trainings while
helping create security plans. MPD participated in Domestic Violence/Intimate
Partner awareness events. Special Events: The Marblehead Police facilitated several
events throughout the year, that included: the Memorial Day Parade, Veteran’s Day
memorial and 911 ceremonies, the Horribles Parade, Christmas parade and tree
lighting, Menorah Lighting, and the Marblehead Festival of Arts Concert series and
programming.

Safe Routes to Schools: Ensuring safe passage to our schools for our students is an
important responsibility for the Marblehead Police, and the locations of the 15 daily
crossing guards on duty. MPD also works on keeping both the students and crossing
guards safe by enhancing signage, crosswalks, and public education, especially
drivers. The Town was awarded a $10,000 lines and signs grant through the SRTS
initiative, which will better our markings, signage and routes to and from schools.
Work was completed prior to the start of the 2024 school year on Village and Maple
Streets. A safer route to schools, with increased signage and sight lines, has been
established.

Technology: Mobile IT - All cruisers now have maintained access to our RMS
system through Mobile CAD and Mobile Reporting, allowing officers full mobile
connectivity while on patrol. IT task force - Continued upgrade/maintenance of
station IT infrastructure. i.e. Migrating to IMC to a new server. Staff attended the
Commonwealth Cybersecurity critical incident response training and seminar.

Marblehead Police UAS (Drone) Unit - The Marblehead Public Safety UAS unit is
officially up and running! with three FAA Part 107 credentialed remote pilots at
MPD: Sgt. Osattin, Officer DiMare, and Officer Rizos. The cost of the unit and
licensing was funded by a donation from a Marblehead resident. The unit conducts
regular training flights to maintain proficiency; public awareness/demonstration
flights (CSI camp); provided operational support and security overflight for numerous
critical public safety events throughout the year (Independence Day, Christmas Tree
Lighting, Christmas Walk, and Santa’s arrival); conducted surveys for other town
departments (Rail Road Right of way for the Planning Department, and the Substation
for Electric Light). The UAS was also utilized for incident management, searches,
SAR, and an apprehension of a criminal suspect that had just committed a crime and
provided thermal imaging to assist response at the July 3rd fireworks barge fire.




                                        219


                                           222
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

All MPD cruisers were outfitted with an Electronic (E) – Citation system, to increase
efficiency and add printing capabilities to ticket writing (paid for by a state grant).

Licensing: In 2024, MPD continued to process and issue licenses for our over 1400
citizens that possess either an LTC or FID card. Our new applicant/renewal
applications are up 25%. Liquor licenses: Sworn personnel serve as licensing officers
as appointed by the Select Board and are responsible for making notifications of
violations. We conducted checks throughout the year, identified potential violations
and communicated with the Select Board for any follow-up actions as needed.

Evidence and Drug Destruction: We have continued to work in conjunction with
the CVS Medication Disposal for Safer Communities program. This program allows
us to safely dispose of unwanted and expired medications (760 lbs). The
property/evidence room has taken in and processed 268 items which range from
found property and booking property, as well as items for criminal prosecution such
as firearms, blood evidence, cash and narcotics to name a few.

Grants: MPD is committed to applying for and managing competitive grants to
improve our department. In FY 24, we were awarded $337,122.00 in grant funds.
These include the Federal Ballistic Vest Reimbursement fund, $5717; State 911
Training and Support Grants – $125,104.00; Municipal Road Safety Grant Program
- $39,999.00 for extra patrols geared at impaired driving and speeding, along with a
speed feedback sign and traffic data box, MPD was awarded $120,000.00 from
Massachusetts Department of Mental Health (DMH) for our civilian mental health
clinician and training. In addition to the SRTS $10,000 grant, we also received grants
for the E-Citation program to install cruiser printers, and an updated electronic
fingerprint interface, and completed several MPD station upgrades with the State
ADA grant funds. MPD assisted both Temple Sinai and Epstein Hillel School with
their successful applications for grant funds under the Executive Office of Public
Safety and Security Nonprofit security grant program.

Training: Training is an area that MPD has committed to expand and increase the
average hours each officer is trained each year. All active duty, sworn police officers,
have been certified by the POST Commission. Additionally, all dispatchers met
yearly dispatch certification requirements. MPD staff also attended several
specialized training courses that are recommended but not required. These trainings
were focused on: Accident reconstruction and analysis, Freedom and Hate Speech,
Suicide intervention and prevention, Electronic Controlled Weapons, Health,
Wellness and Resilience, internal affairs investigations, Gung Crime intelligence,
Recovery Coaching, Emergency Management Planning for Schools and Leadership.
I’d like to highlight the training provided by NERAC for active shooter response,
which focused on Rescue Task Forces, and breeching skills. MPD employees also



                                         220


                                       223
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

contributed maximum points to the Town’s MIIA insurance for additional training
completed. MPD implemented a formal Field Training Officer program in 2021. In
2024 we had one officer (C. Hennigar) graduate from the Lynnfield Police Academy
and was introduced to the program. Field training is very important to acclimatize
new officers to the Town of Marblehead and transition them from an Academy setting
into providing the highest standard of professional policing tailored to the citizens of
Marblehead.

Personnel: There were notable personnel changes in 2024. Sgt. Jason Conrad was
promoted to Lieutenant and Officer Tim Morley was promoted to Sergeant. One new
hire, Christian Hennigar, joined the department and is working full time, while a
recruit, Taylor Nolasco, started the academy in September. Despite a greater call
volume, forced shifts were limited, and staffing patrol operations maintained. Officer
Luke Peters resigned to pursue other avenues; we thank him for his years of service.
Part time Dispatcher Marianne Kelleher also resigned; we wish her well.

Joint Dispatch Center: The MPD/MFD Dispatch team stood out again in 2024.
Dispatch Supervisor Amy Gilliland was named Dispatcher Leader of the year! The
Dispatch “GIS Dashboard” has greatly expanded and has garnered attention from
Public Safety and ESRI personnel. This interactive ArcGIS Online Dashboard
provides instant access to vital information that Dispatchers use when dispatching
first responders and other personnel. When accessed, it can triangulate callers'
locations, reduce response times, and increase multi-agency coordination to minimize
property damage, streamline municipal resources and save lives during emergencies.
After reviewing our progress, Esri committed to free licensing for another year,
saving the Town almost $50,000.

Miscellaneous: I’d like to identify a few other services provided by MPD in 2024
that can go unnoticed: MPD processed over 1100 Public Records requests, and 90
Notary Public requests. MPD works tirelessly with stakeholders to address those
suffering from substance use disorders and mental health issues. We continue to
support community conversations on substance use disorder awareness and
education. MPD stands out against all forms of hate and bias and works with our
community partners to prepare for and react to incidents of violence, or threats of
violence. Many of these efforts are with the TFAD, whose members are dedicated to
addressing discrimination. Mental Health Task Force – As a member of the
Marblehead Mental Health Task Force, MPD participates supports their effort to help
those suffering with mental illness. The Town supported the use of ARPA funds to
replace our failing back up emergency generator. This replacement will allow for all
our systems, many of which are the Town’s core communication systems, to stay
online when needed most. The Marblehead Police Department, and individual
members, received a few awards in 2024 for our efforts. To include JCC’s Annual

                              ANIMAL CONTROL:

The Marblehead Animal Control Department had another busy year in 2024. We saw
an increase in our need to help with orphaned wildlife and transporting them to
wildlife hospitals. We worked with the community to educate them on living with our
wildlife neighbors and solving conflict in a humane manner. We, as in other
communities, are seeing an increase in domestic animals being abandoned.

Dogs licensed: 3,031. Dogs brought to the Animal Control Building including
protective custody 15. Returned to the owners 10 Adopted through the shelter 4
Cats brought to the Animal Control Building 6 Returned to the owners 2 Adopted out
through the shelter 4 Tortoise brought to the Animal Control Building 1 Returned to
the owner: Shelter Adoptions Cats/Kittens 105, Dogs 3, Rat 1, Guinea Pig 1, Bird 1

Violation/citations/warnings/investigated: Leash: 375, Pooper Scooper 5, Barking
45, License 25, Failure to Vaccinate for Rabies 5, Seasonal 38, quarantines 24, and
animal bites.

Calls directly into the Animal Control Department for Lost or Found Pets, Animal
complaints for domestic and wildlife 526; calls directly into MPD Dispatch – 827.

We would like to thank Atlantic Vet Hospital, Our Family Veterinary Services and
all our volunteers who make our job possible!

Since Marblehead has a 24/7 leach law with over 3,000 dogs licensed in town, we are
hoping to create an additional dog park and another area where dogs will be able to

                       RECREATION AND PARKS
OVERVIEW
The Recreation and Parks Commission submits this report for the year ending
December 31, 2024.

The Commission is made up of five residents who are elected annually at a general
town election in June. The Commission’s scheduled meetings are held on the first
and third Tuesday of each month at 6:00 PM in the Community Center at 10
Humphrey Street. These meetings consist of reports, the appearance of persons or
groups on the agenda, the review of correspondence submitted for consideration,
discussions of plans and objectives, and reviews of policies, rules, and regulations as
needed. The Commission reviews and approves the hiring of personnel when
recommendations are presented. Budgets are reviewed by the Commission annually
and they act as an advocate with the Finance Committee and at Town Meeting.

2024 SUMMARY AND HIGHLIGHTS

A. PARKS

Fields and School Athletic Facility Maintenance

The Department maintained the fields and is responsible for the field preparation,
including lines, for the following sports:
     • High School (Varsity, Junior Varsity, and Frosh) football, baseball, softball,
         lacrosse, soccer, and field hockey;
     • Youth Sports baseball, softball, lacrosse, soccer, and football;
     • Men’s softball and soccer, and co-ed softball.

Fields opened for the season in early April 2024. The spring sports required the lining
of fields for varsity school sports first. Then followed by youth sports, mid-April
through the summer; adult softball through September; followed by Varsity/JV
soccer, field hockey, and football at the end of August. In September, October, and
November, all varsity, junior varsity, and middle school football, soccer, field hockey
fields, and youth sports fields were maintained for their seasons.

The mowing and trimming of athletic fields is done on weekdays with school in
session due to extensive usage on afternoons, evenings, and weekends. Line marking
with latex paint and/or non-caustic chalk is also done while classes are inside the
schools. Mowing of properties began in early April and continued through November
on a five day per week schedule. The new turf and field at Piper opened in August




                                         227


                                            230
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

2024. Leaf cleanups ran October through December, and spring cleanups ran March
through May.

Budget and staff limitations, requests for extra work, and changes on short notice
remained a continual problem. Early spring and late fall field scheduling depended
on when fields were dormant, wet, frozen, or overused in any one area. Fall remains
the best time to do seeding and sod work on fields but was frequently in conflict with
the demands of the activities scheduled and the department’s continual shortage of
funds, materials, and manpower versus those demands.

All line marking, mowing, and field schedule changes were coordinated by
Superintendent Peter James. In the summer and fall, employees maintained the lines
on high school and youth sports fields. These efforts continually earned praise from
the school athletic department and youth sports leagues. The field paint budget
continues to be strained by additional field requests and rising paint costs.

During the year the Sports Committee met at the Community Center with Linda Rice
Collins and Shelly Bedrossian acting as liaison for the Commission twice during the
year. Throughout the year, there was an open line of communication with the Public
Schools, Board of Health, and sports organizations to help ensure various town sports
teams and coaches could minimize conflicts and maximize usage.
The Departments budget was reduced in fiscal year 2024 but service demands
remained the same.

Field and Property Permitting and Management

The Commission received numerous requests for fundraising and business use of the
Community Center and the parks. These requests must be approved by a vote of the
Commission as required in the Town By-laws.

Building, Parks, and Facility Remodel and Maintenance

Stramski Building Renovation
The Recreation & Parks continues overseeing renovations on the Stramski Building
for recreation and sailing programs. The Department is working with Essex Tech
carpentry, electrical, and plumbing programs to frame, wire and renovate the interior.
The renovated building will have an event space with a catering kitchen and two
meeting rooms on the second floor.

Hobbs Playground Signage
In partnership with the Marblehead Family Fund and the Power of Speech, the
Department created and installed Communication Boards for Hobbs playground that



                                        230


                                       233
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

will allow a more inclusive environment for the Town’s children. The recently
renovated playground, remains a favorite of the town’s youngest children and has
become wildly popular with its new positive, inclusive, and fun environment that
inspires everyone to learn, imagine, and play together. In addition to the boards at
Hobbs Playground, there were a total of six boards installed at playgrounds across the
Town of Marblehead.

Tennis & Pickleball Courts
The Department continues to maintain and update its Outdoor Racket Court Policies
based on community usage of courts and feedback. The Department launched a NEW
Seaside Park Pickleball Courts Open Play system. Open Social Play is available daily
on Courts C & D from 4:00 PM - Close.

Memorial Benches
The maintenance and upkeep of memorial benches, continues to be a challenge for
the department. A moratorium on new Memorial Benches was put in place. Current
benches maintained by the department are located at Chandler Hovey, Castle Rock,
Memorial Park, Crocker Park, Seaside Park, Gatchell’s Playground, Fountain Park,
Stramski and Redd’s Pond.

Rail Trails
The department continues to work collaboratively with the Marblehead Conservancy
to maintain 5 miles of trails and 150 acres. Department provides labor, support and
tools as requested and needed through January 25, 2032.

Additional Park and Property Renovations and Upgrades
Renovations continue on several properties in town. The department also made
upgrades or repairs throughout the year as needed to park and town properties,
including:

    1.   Devereux Beach, new beach pavilion built with new plastic picnic tables that
         can be easily removed for storms and end of season. Pavilion was a great
         addition this season for rentals and for hosting our Department’s public
         events, including the Pirate Adventure.
    2.   Hammond Nature Center, repair retaining wall
    3.   Green Street, light replacement and fix
    4.   Gatchell’s, light replacement
    5.   Riverhead, replacement shed installed in the early season
    6.   Town Playgrounds, installation of community boards at six playgrounds
         across the town in partnership with Marblehead Family Fund.

Building and Comfort Station (Restroom) Maintenance

In addition to the Community Center, the department oversees other buildings and
facilities throughout the town. The department continuously maintained a workshop
and equipment storage at Devereux Beach, maintenance garage on Vine Street,
Reynolds playground storage room, Gerry (Stramski’s) locker room and sailing
classroom, Hamond Nature Center, and the Seaside Park grandstand storage area.

The department-maintained comfort stations (restrooms) at the following locations:
Chandler Hovey Park, Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Fort Sewall, Reynolds
Playground, Gerry Playground, Gatchell’s Playground, and Hamond Nature Center
on a seasonal basis. Water and sewer costs, electricity, cleaning supplies, and paper
goods came from the department budget. Restrooms at Devereux Beach, Chandler
Hovey, Fort Sewall and Crocker Park were maintained throughout the season by an
external cleaning company on the weekends. Through the day, restrooms were
maintained by department staff. The department completed the winterizing of all
comfort stations in early November.

Doggie bags were provided through the town’s budget at Crocker Park, Redd’s Pond,
Fort Sewall and the Dog Park. Dogs are prohibited from all athletic fields and
children’s playgrounds. All other Parks require dogs to be leashed. The Department
is reviewing a Pooch Pass program that would allow more space for off leash area
and play for dogs and additional disposal receptacles.

Equipment Replacement and Seasonal Staffing
The Department replaced the gas boilers at the Jacobi Community Center.

The department continued to look for reliable seasonal maintenance staff from April
through November. The Department’s mechanic also retired and the department is
filling the position for 2025.

Facility & Grounds Landscape Maintenance Program

trees and shrubs, and removal of litter on the following properties in 2024. (Note: “*”
denotes athletic fields requiring continuous mowing, lining, and reconditioning for
the Town’s sports programs):

  4.   SUMMER Programs – Indoors and Outdoors (June – August)

       Summer Program Particapants by the Numbers
       • Playground Programs: 1,300
       • EAST COAST SUP Partnership Programming: 850
       • Youth Sailing: 275
       • Tennis Programs: 250
       • All Other Programming: 1,830

       Summer Long Programs

       Playground Program
       Marblehead Recreation & Parks signature playground program welcomed
       more than 150 children each week—more than 1,300 participants
       throughout the summer—running from June 24 through August 23, 2024.
       The Director of the Playground Program and counselors provided




                                     238
                MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
__________________________________________________________________

      programming and staffing for 1,300 kids ages 4.5-Grade 11 over nine weeks.
      For the second year, the Department added a ninth week of the program
      to meet the needs of community members and families. Highlights of the
      summer featured unique activities – fire truck cool downs and mini-golf.

      Sailing Programs – Adult and Youth
      Marblehead Recreation & Parks offers sailing programs for more than 275
      adults and youth grades 3-9. It’s designed for sailors of all levels of sailing
      experience to build confidence to feel comfortable and enjoy being out on
      the water! Class curriculum includes on land and on the water coaching that
      teaches water safety and basic sailing techniques. The youth program is
      offered for seven weeks 9-3 PM and the adult programs are offered on
      evenings throughout the summer.

      Tennis Programs
      The Department Tennis Programs served up fun and skills for more than 250
      youth participants throughout the summer.

      Tennis 4 Kids: our beginner tennis program is a fun and engaging way to
      introduce kids to the sport of tennis for ages 4 – Grade 4.

      Tennis Match Play: participants improved tennis skills and strokes with
      players of similar abilities for grades 4-7.
      East Coast Style SUP
      The partnership with East Coast SUP provided programming to more than
      850 participants throughout the summer. FUN and safety were the two main
      ingredients in all of East Coast Style SUP programs, available in full, half
      day, or hourly class programs from ages 3 through adult.

2024 Public Events & Initiatives
The Department launched a series of new and annually beloved events and programs
in 2024 to meet the needs of our residents and families highlighted below:

NEW! Ski Bradford – Weekly Ski & Ride Program
The Recreation & Parks Department launched a NEW six-week program on Tuesday
afternoons to Bradford Ski Mountain. Building on the success of the one-day ski trips
to Bradford in 2023 and 2022, every Tuesday, 40 kids in grades 3-7 packed onto a
Healey Bus, charted by Marblehead Recreation & Parks. The Department was able
to lead and kick off the winter 6-week program because of high interest in our one-
day ski programs as well as the driver availability of charter school buses through
Healey Bus company. Each week Jan-March, 40 participants, 1 Rec & Park staff
member and 3 parent volunteers traveled to the mountain for an evening of energetic
skiing and outdoor winter fun!

Ski Bradford One Day Trip – Sunday, January 21
The Recreation & Parks Department organized the third annual a one-day ski trip to
Bradford Mountain on a weekend in January. Participants in grades 3-6 joined the
Marblehead Recreation & Parks team for a day of fun with new and old friends on
the slopes. We worked with the Marblehead Public School Department to secure a
town bus and driver to bring more than 26 participants—from beginners to young
meisters—and six chaperones join staff members for fun, skiing, and a winter treat!

Sweetheart Dance – Friday, April 5
Nearly 400 children and their special sweetheart attended this sold-out event with a
live DJ, dancing, dessert decorating and more! Tickets sold out quickly and it was a
highly coveted event!

An Egg-citing Egg Drop Challenge with Marblehead Fire Department – Sunday,
April 7
Marblehead Recreation & Parks and the Marblehead Fire Department teamed up to
provide the widely popular “Fire Truck Egg Drop Challenge”, during which children
designed contraptions to protect a raw egg as it was dropped from the top of 50-foot
lift. The event drew nearly 80 children ages 3 years old - sixth grade to enter the egg
drop challenge. The ingenuity of the designs was diverse and creative. Kids created
vessels out of boxes, cotton balls, straws, balloons, and even soccer balls to protect a
raw egg as it was dropped from the top of a 50-foot lift by firefighters from the
Marblehead Fire Department. One of the Fire Department’s ladder trucks was out of
commission, so backup was needed, and the eggs were dropped from a JLG lift.
However, the absence of a ladder truck for the drop challenge did not make it any less




                                        243
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

exciting. Each participant was awarded with a small prize for taking part in the
challenge, and those whose eggs survived the freefall were awarded with an egg
trophy.

April Vacation Club @ Gatchell’s
This Spring program ran during Marblehead Public Schools April break for 30
students in grades K-5. Coach Olivia and Marblehead Recreation & Parks staff
provided a fun-filled week of games, sports, and arts and crafts at Gatchell’s Park.

mARRblehead Pirate Adventure @ Devereux Beach* - Friday, July 19
Marblehead Recreation & Parks transformed Devereux Beach into a swashbuckling
adventure and treasure hunt! At this sold out event, more than 100 little mates kicked
off their summer weekend with a pirate journey! Buccaneers picked up a pirate hat
and treasure map. Then, young pirates ran off to seek out pirate booty and adventure!
DevereuX marked the spot for this mARRR-blehead pirate adventure!

No School Day @ Gatchell’s! – October 3 & 4
Fifty students in grades K-6 joined the Recreation & Parks playground staff for fun-
filled day of games, sports, and arts and crafts! This program was run during the
Marblehead Public School holiday.

NEW! Haunted Hamond Hospital – Saturday, October 26
Marblehead Recreation & Parks partnered with local programs leaders to bring a
Haunted Hamond Hospital to Marblehead. The packed event drew a large crowd
eager to “get a check up” at the eerie and uncanny experience put on by haunted tour
guides of all ages. The success of this 2024 event has already sparked planning for a
haunted adventure in 2025!

NEW! November No School Days – November 12-26
Recreation & Parks Department pivoted with community need organized and
provided a structured program for kids in grades K-6 during the days Marblehead
Public Schools were closed due to the teacher strike. The Department ran ELEVEN
fun-filled days at Clifton Lutheran Church with four high school staff members and
Playground Director Olivia Ballard. Activities included outdoor sports, visits to
Hobbs playground, arts & crafts, games and breakfast and lunch were provided for
participants by Marblehead Public School food services team.

2024 Scarecrow Stroll – Saturday, October 19
More than 1,000 children, families, and community members strolled and trick-or-
treated through festive scarecrow designs. The 2024 Scarecrow Stroll drew the most




                                           244
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

scarecrows yet – more than 60 - from businesses, scout groups, and individuals.
Highlights included a giant horse scarecrow from Crosby’s, Lacrosse and other sports
program themed scarecrows, and eager children enjoying cider from the Landing
Restaurant.

C. NEW MARBLEHEAD RECREATION & PARKS ONLINE SERVICES

Court Reservation Systems:
    • Tennis Drop-In Courts
    • Pickleball Drop-in Courts
    • Badminton Drop-in Indoor Courts
Marblehead Recreation & Parks continues to utilize the online reservation system –
SuperSaaS - to accommodate ease of registration.

Permit Application Process:
The application and insurance permitting process, as well as the Kayak, Float and
Pram Permit process continue to be streamlined online for Marblehead Recreation &
Parks properties including Devereux Beach, Crocker Park, Chandler Hovey Park,
Community Center room rentals, Riverhead Beach, Stramski, and other department
properties and special requests.

NEW Beach Parking System:
In 2024, Marblehead recreation & Park implemented a new credit card payment
system for beach parking. The Department used Square software for payments this
year. The split between payment taken from credit cards vs. cash was almost 50/50.

                  SHELLFISH CONSTABLE REPORT

The contiguous beach which ranges from the end of Gallison Avenue to the end of
the Veterans Memorial Causeway, known as Devereux, Tucker’s, and Goldthwait
beaches, were opened for the harvesting of surf clams on October 1, 2024.
Marblehead is issued a conditional license to open our fishery by the Division of
Marine Fisheries on an annual basis. The clamming season in Marblehead is
historically during the months of October through April. The areas may open later
and/or close temporarily during the season when storms and environmental issues
arise. As of the writing of this report, there have been 64 licenses issued.

Monitoring clammers, checking water quality and testing of the clams is performed
regularly by the Massachusetts Division of Marine Fisheries and residents that have
volunteered to serve as Shellfish Constables. Because of the diligence of our
Constables, we have a very healthy fishery with many undersized clams paving the
way for “keepers” in the following years. The primary shellfish in this location is the
Atlantic Surf Clam (Spisula solidissima).

A license is required for anyone wishing to harvest shellfish and licenses must be
obtained through the Town Clerk’s office at Abbot Hall.

Respectfully submitted, John G. Attridge, Chief Shellfish Constable

              The Town was awarded and began development of the State Street Landing and
              Harbormaster’s Tucker’s Wharf Coastal Resilience Project through a $210,502.40
              CZM Coastal Resilience Grant. This effort builds on conceptual adaptation designs
              generated through the Town’s FY20 CZM grant. The project area includes Town-
              owned waterfront properties that are vital to commercial and recreational boating,
              public open space, residential use, and the Harbormaster’s Office. The
              Massachusetts Coastal Flood Risk Model identifies this area as one of five priority
              zones within Marblehead Harbor at high risk for coastal flooding. This project
              includes a detailed analysis of coastal processes and vulnerabilities through the
              years 2030, 2050, and 2070, and will refine adaptation strategies and preferred
              implementation pathways. The Town contributed $29,216.08 in in-kind services to
              support this effort.

              Through the Metropolitan Area Planning Council’s (MAPC) Technical Assistance
              Program, the Town was awarded $25,000 for the Sustainable Heritage Grant
              Project. In collaboration with the Old and Historic Districts Commission and the
              Green Marblehead Implementation Committee, the project developed practical,
              context-sensitive recommendations that integrate environmental sustainability with
              historic preservation goals. The initiative aims to improve energy efficiency, reduce

       In support of long-term planning, the Town was awarded $130,000 from the
       Executive Office of Housing and Livable Communities through the Community
       Planning Grant Program to begin updating Marblehead’s Comprehensive Master
       Plan. In collaboration with MAPC, the Town will undertake a 12–18 month process
       to update the plan, originally adopted in 1989. The Town is contributing $13,000 in
       matching funds.

       To improve accessibility, the Town secured $92,028 from the Massachusetts Office
       on Disability through the Municipal ADA Improvement Grant Program. These
       funds will support ADA upgrades at Devereux Beach, including improvements to
       the parking lot, restroom facilities, and walkways, as prioritized in the Town’s 2023
       ADA Transition Plan. This project was fully funded through the grant, with no local
       match required.

       Additionally, the Town was awarded $8,500 through the Department of Industrial
       Accidents to promote worker safety. The grant funded a bucket truck safety training
       session for Department of Public Works employees. The project was fully grant-
       funded with no local match required.

           TASK FORCE AGAINST DISCRIMINATION
In the summer of 1989, a series of hate crimes horrified Marblehead and resulted in
community outrage and condemnation. A group of concerned citizens requested that
the Board of Selectmen appoint a task force in response. As a result, on August 2,
1989, the Marblehead Task Force Against Discrimination (TFAD) was established.

The TFAD updated mission statement is: “The Marblehead Task Force Against
Discrimination is committed to ensuring that Marblehead is a respectful, supportive,
welcoming, and inclusive community for its many diverse citizens and visitors.”

To accomplish its mission, TFAD responds to acts of discrimination, bigotry, hate,
and intolerance; expresses the community’s condemnation of such acts; collaborates
with community groups to provide programs combating discrimination and
promoting respect; and seeks to support those affected by discrimination and hatred
in Marblehead.

                 The Task Force would like all citizens to know about the work of
                 TFAD and how to reach out for help if needed. For this reason, the
                 Task Force continues to be grateful for the Town Moderator’s
                 permission to display Marblehead’s No Place for Hate banner at
                 Town Meeting.

The Task Force Against Discrimination returned to holding their
monthly meetings in person. The Task Force is fortunate to have
Shari Pressman, an Observer from the League of Women Voters
at each meeting who records and submits the minutes to the local
press. The Marblehead Task Force Against Discrimination and
the Marblehead Racial Justice Team would like to thank the
citizens of this magnificent town for promoting diversity, equity,
and inclusion. During this past year, the citizens have held a
town-wide celebration of the LGBTQIA+ community, have
raised the Juneteenth flag to fly over our Town Hall, and honored Transgender
members of our community for their courage regarding visibility and acceptance.
Marblehead Pride stickers, which Task Force member Reece Dahlberg created,
were also made available. We attended the 6th Annual Indigenous People’s Day in
the gardens of Jeremiah Lee Mansion. Additionally, along with a proclamation by
the Select Board, TFAD observed the Second Annual International Holocaust
Remembrance Day, a commemoration declared by the United Nations in 2005.




                                      253
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

During Black History Month and in conjunction with February School Vacation,
TFAD offered the picture book Sit-In: How Four Friends Stood Up by Sitting Down
by Angela Davis Pinkney. The books were donated by the Marblehead Public Schools
and other community members.

In May, TFAD celebrated Asian American and Pacific Islander Heritage Month,
chaired by TFAD member Kim Gubelman. This entertaining and educational
program showcased the rich cultural heritage of the AAPI community and included
a variety of artistic performances including dragon dancing, martial arts, and poetry
readings. The event was met with great enthusiasm and was instrumental in forging
connections within our community, shining a light on the importance of diversity
and cultural appreciation while honoring the local Asian community.




As part of its ongoing efforts to raise awareness, TFAD celebrated their 35th
Anniversary by holding several enjoyable activities over the weekend of September
7-8th. On Saturday, TFAD had a booth at the Marblehead Farmers Market where
they distributed leaflets which detail its mission and actions as well as resources
that can help citizens and visitors in Marblehead reach out for help and report hate
crimes and violence. TFAD’s “No Place for Hate” stickers were also distributed by
TFAD members who were available to speak with community members and visitors
while raising awareness about TFAD and its work in the community. Additionally,
there was an opportunity to participate in a craft project, which was offered to
youngsters to color a drawing “Walk In My Shoes” (picture included below). On
Sunday, TFAD sponsored two events appropriate for all age groups, from school
children to adults. The movie Wonder was shown at the Warwick Theater, followed
by Latin dancing with professional instructor Greg Coles and Israeli dancing led by
Lainie Goodman at the Judy and Gene Jacobi Senior Center.




                                           254
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________


There continues to be an unprecedented rise in antisemetic incidents. Chief King and
Marblehead law enforcement have been vigilant in protecting the Jewish institutions
and houses of worship. We appreciate all that TFAD member (ex-officio) and Chief
of Police Dennis King along with the officers on his staff have accomplished to
protect our citizens and the work they have done to investigate the incidents that have
occurred throughout the year.

Marblehead is a “No Place for Hate” community, a designation earned through the
Anti-Defamation League. Incidents of racism, ageism, sexism, anti-Semitism,
ableism, Islamophobia, and homophobia run counter to that designation. Marblehead
citizens will find our “No Place for Hate” stickers displayed on over 100 businesses
in town and on the doors of schools, places of worship, and other public spaces. These
stickers are a reminder that hatred, bigotry, and discrimination of any kind will not
be tolerated.

We would like to remind all our fellow citizens that, in addition to the Task Force
Against Discrimination, there are many resources available if you witness an
incident or are the target of hatred of any kind. These resources include:

         Police: suspected violations of the hate crimes law should be reported to the
         police in the first instance so you can be informed of your rights. Please
         contact the Marblehead Police (781)-631-1212.

         Attorney General Andrea Campbell: The AG has established a hotline
         managed by attorneys and staff from the AG’s office. While not every
         incident will be appropriate for legal action, the AG’s office tracks reports
         and appropriate matters may be referred to local law enforcement or the
         Attorney General’s Criminal Bureau 1-800-994-3228.

         The Task Force Against Discrimination: Please reach us through the
         Selectmen’s office at 781-631-0000 or wileyk@marblehead.org.

TFAD acknowledges Niko King and new member Maren Potter for their role as
Marblehead High School student representatives. The Task Force Against
Discrimination bids adieu to Kim Gubelman and Scott Marcus. We welcomed new
member Jean Guastaferri.

The Task Force Against Discrimination continues to call on all Marblehead residents
to join with the Task Force in its ongoing efforts to ensure that Marblehead is a

                            VETERANS’ AGENT

MISSION STATEMENT

THE MARBLEHEAD VETERANS SERVICES OFFICE WILL ASSIST AND
SERVE ALL VETERANS AND THEIR FAMILIES WITH ALL VETERAN
RELATED MATTERS. THE VETERANS OFFICE WILL GO OVER AND
ABOVE TO HELP VETERANS/THEIR FAMILIES IN NEED IN MARBLEHEAD.

DEPARTMENT DESCRIPTION

Marblehead Veteran Services assists in applying for federal benefits through the
Veteran’s Administration. These benefits include enrolling in the VA HealthCare
System, applying for a service-connected disability or VA Pension, Aid and
Attendance, and much more. In some cases, there will be benefits for the surviving
family members when their Veteran passes. The Commonwealth of MA offers state
Veteran benefits, through MGL Chap. 115. Chapter 115 Benefits are financial,
medical, and service benefits to qualified Veterans and their dependents who meet
the income guidelines. Other local benefits include property tax exemptions and
abatements, education, annuities, job training and mental health counseling, to name
a few. We also assist with Food Gift Cards to help Veterans cover the ever-rising
cost of groceries. Currently, there are 7 Veterans who receive Chap. 115 Benefits in
Marblehead. In the last several months, this office has completed and submitted
approximately 45 VA Claims for Veterans for disabilities stemming from their time
in the Armed Forces. This doesn’t include the many other benefits that have been
applied for.

This office also assists and conducts funeral services along with local chaplains and
VFW Post 2005. All those who served on active duty are entitled to Military Honors.

Yearly, this Agent attends all required Federal, State and Association trainings and
passes all required Certification Exams. Executive Office of Veteran Services has
implemented a “Learning Management Training” curriculum for agents to complete
and pass.

This office has at a minimum, two mandatory ceremonies per year: Memorial Day
and Veterans Day. This office will continue to keep the traditions Marblehead holds
near and dear to their heart for these ceremonies, as well as bringing in some new
ones.




                                      257
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Due to the passing of two very important pieces of Legislation, The PACT Act and
The HERO Act, the number of Veterans and their families who we serve has grown.

We have teamed up with the COA to do regular get-togethers for Veterans, which has
helped to spread the word about the increase in VA Benefits. We have also
outreached to the various other organizations and resources Marblehead has, to better
assist our Veterans.

VSO (Ret.) Dave Rodgers proudly served the Veterans of Marblehead for over 25
years. He honored and helped hundreds of Veterans and their family members
through the years and continues to help them. I truly appreciate the work he has done
before me and how he continues to mentor me in my role here as Marblehead’s newest
VSO.


Respectfully submitted,
Roseann Trionfi-Mazzuchelli
Veteran Service Officer

                WATER AND SEWER COMMISSION

The Water and Sewer Commission is a five-member elected board, members of which
serve three-year alternating terms. Monthly public meetings are typically held on the
first Tuesday of each month. A public rate setting hearing is convened every year. In
2024, the Public Rate Hearing was held in June. The Commission oversees the Water
Department and the Sewer Department, both of which are enterprise funded.

                               The Commission

                               It is with heavy hearts that the Commission informs
                               all of the untimely passing of Commissioner Thomas
                               Carroll. Mr. Carroll was a dedicated member of the
                               Commission for 12 years and will be remembered
                               fondly. He was a valued member of the Commission
                               with his many years working in the water and sewer
                               industry. The knowledge he gathered on water
                               distribution systems across the country while selling
                               American Darling Hydrants and Valves was a
                               tremendous asset in developing the capital
                               improvement projects for both Water and Sewer. All
                               who attended Commission meetings during Mr.
                               Carrolls tenure realized quickly the benefits
                               Marblehead gained from his expertise, passion,
                               understanding, and leadership.


Mr. Carrol sat on the Commission from 2002 to 2006 and again from 2019 to 2024.
During his time as Commissioner, many exemplary projects were completed. The
installation of the new storm water system in the shipyard district immediately
relieved that district of historical flooding and provided a system that was large
enough to handle the improvements made years later with the Pleasant Street
Drainage project and future projects under development. Some improvements made
to the water distribution system were the improvements to both booster pump stations
through upgraded pumps, Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition system, and
generators with the capacity to run the entire station during times of power outages.
Improvements to the sewer collection system were seen through the rehabilitation of
the Sargent Road and Green Street sewer pump stations and the creation and
implementation of the Infiltration and Inflow plan.




                                      259
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Mr. Carroll strived to protect the rate payer while ensuring the departments had
adequate funds to provide a quality service and product. His guidance and leadership
will provide a standard for the Water and Sewer Departments to follow for years to
come.
                                Highlights of 2024
                                       Water
Marblehead received approximately 46 inches of rain during 2024. This resulted in
increased water use which can be attributed to the many irrigation systems throughout
Town. The Commission encourages all residents to review their lawn maintenance
plans and use best practices to conserve water. GREENSCAPES North Shore
Coalition is an excellent resource for lawn care and gardening guidance:
www.greenscapes.org

                                         Sewer
The Sewer department continues to make strides in the infiltration and inflow removal
program. Inflow is where clear groundwater enters the sanitary sewer system through
illegal connections. Illegal connections that have been found in Marblehead are sump
pumps that are tied to the sanitary sewer line directly with piping, or indirectly with
hoses into slop sinks or floor drains tied into the sanitary sewer system. In 2024,
twenty sump pumps were found. This number of pumps can theoretically put as much
water into the system while they are working as 500 homes discharging wastewater.
We all need to work together to eliminate these connections and if you are not sure
please give us a call and we will inspect your system and let you know if the setup is
correct at no cost to you. The cost of collecting and treating storm water that goes into
sanitary system is passed on to all sewer rate payers.
If you would like further information on how you can minimize potential
issues caused by Infiltration and Inflow, visit www.RESUMP.org for more
information.

                       Capital Improvements Projects
                                        Water
Contract 190 – Water Main Improvements
In 2024, the Commission began a multiyear water main improvement project.
This contract was publicly bid and awarded to N Granese & Sons. The main focus
in the first year was to improve water quality and increase fire-flow by replacing
6-inch watermains. Replacement of 6” unlined cast iron pipe with 8” cement lined
ductile iron pipe was completed on West Street, Mystic Road, Florence Road,
Maverick Street, and Stony Brook Road.

                                      Sewer
Contract 183 – Pump Station Service Replacement
This contract was awarded to Dagle Electrical Construction Corporation and began
in 2023, supply chain issues have caused major delays in completing this project.
Completion of upgrades at the Village, Nahant, Harbor, and Sumac Pump stations
will be early 2025. These projects increase safety and ease of emergency power
generation.

Contract 184 – Green Street Pump Station Rehabilitation
Rehabilitation work at Green Street pump station began in 2023 and was completed
in 2024. This contract saw significant delays in components needed for completion.
N. Granese and Sons was awarded the project through the public bidding process.
The new equipment installed includes new pumps and valves, new VFD’s, electrical
panels, HVAC system, SCADA system, automatic transfer switch, and upgraded
safety measures.




                                      261
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Contract 191 – Sewer Rehabilitation
Following the infiltration and inflow plan developed in response to an EPA
Administrative Order issued to the town in 2020, the Commission developed and
procured a multi-year sewer rehabilitation contract which was awarded to National
Water Main Corporation. This contract includes the lining of sewer mains and
manholes. Year 1 started in late 2024 with manhole rehabilitation in the Clifton
Pump Station District. The sewer lining portion of the contract will start early 2025.
Areas to be lined were chosen through extensive investigations including flow
metering, smoke testing, review of historic records and video inspections.

                          Regulatory Requirements
                          Water - PWS ID 3168000
In 2021, the US EPA published revisions to the Lead and Copper Rule that are
intended to better protect children and communities from lead exposure risks and
support efforts to remove lead from drinking water. As part of the revisions, all water
systems were required to complete an inventory documenting the material of every
water service line. The Water department completed the inventory of 7975 service
lines in October 2024




A grant under the Drinking Water State Revolving Fund (DWSRF) was awarded to
the Commission that provided 100% of the cost reimbursement for developing the
water service line inventory and lead service line replacement plan. The Department
worked with Haley Ward to complete the tasks of reviewing existing records
that contain information on material on the public and/or private side of each
service, created an inventory form for the public to complete a self-investigation of
the private side of their service while also offering appointments for the water
department to complete this survey, excavation at multiple water service
line locations of undocumented material were also completed.

Although efforts are still ongoing to identify every service line material, the
Commission has found no record of lead service lines.




                                       263
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

Property Owners whose water line material that was still unknown or consisted
of galvanized iron received a letter explaining the next steps the Water Department
will take and how homeowners can assist.

Any Resident who received this letter or who would like information regarding their
water service line should email Water@marblehead.org be sure to include your
address and contact information in the request.

In addition to daily monitoring of water quality, the Water Department collected over
300 water samples that were tested for coliform and other bacterial organisms to
ensure that the quality of water provided meets U.S Environmental Protection Agency
(EPA) and Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection (Mass DEP)
drinking water standards.
Lead and Copper samples from 20 residences and two schools were tested. Results
from 2024 were once again below the action level set by the Mass DEP.

In 2024, a multiyear contract was awarded and work started on Infiltration and Inflow

The Sanitary Sewer Overflow (SSO) Notification Plan included the purchase and

near

water resources of Marblehead. Information on water quality and active

                           WATER DEPARTMENT
Marblehead received an average of 1.74 million gallons/day (MGD) of water from
Massachusetts Water Resource Authority (MWRA) in 2024. This is significantly
more than the previous year’s average of 1.67 million gallons/day (MGD). The
department operates and maintains the distribution system, which consists of over
100 miles of pipe, two pump stations, and a water storage tank. The eight-member
crew repairs water main breaks, completes hydrant maintenance on over 890
hydrants, conducts biannual hydrant flushing, read and installs water meters, collects
water samples, and responds to all emergencies and customer service calls 24/7. The
crew assists residents with water usage issues and responds to any questions they may
have concerning their water. Employees trained in water sampling monitor the water
entering the system 365 days a year.

Emergency Response
The Water Department responded to 8 water main break repairs. The Water
Department’s emergency planning and training provided limited to no interruptions
of water delivery during the repair work.
In 2024, the Water Department responded to 130 emergency dig safes requests. This
requires marking out all water infrastructure in the requested area within 1 hour.
Requests are received 24 hours a day/ 7 days a week.

The crew responds to all requests for pipe identification from contractors excavating
in Marblehead.

                          SEWER DEPARTMENT

The sewer department operates and maintains the sewer collection system, which
consist of 28 pump stations, over 100 miles of pipe, and over 3,000 manholes. The
eight-member crew inspects, repairs, and maintains the entire collection system;
including sewer main cleaning, root control, fats oil and grease removal, daily pump
station inspections, and preventative maintenance. The crew responds to all
emergencies and customer service calls 24/7.

Marblehead sent an average of 1.98 million gallons per day (MGD) of sewage with
an average of 77 lb/day of Total Suspended Solids loading and an average of 2,880
lb/day Biological Oxygen Demand loadings to South Essex Sewerage District.
Following the Infiltration and Inflow plan, the Commission has inspected 5,904
properties and 546 illegal sump pump connections have been removed.

Emergency Response
Crowninshield Road pump station experienced a power interruption due to the
electrical supply line failure. The department utilizing a portable generator kept the
station working while Marblehead Electric Light completed the service line
replacement.

The Norman Street pump station had a sewer force main break. The Sewer
department set up a bypass of the station upon notification of sanitary sewer overflow
and continued bypass operations under emergency work status. N. Granese & Sons
completed the repair.




                                           266
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024

In addition to the Normans Street Pump Stations Sanitary Sewer Overflow
(SSO), the department responded to and corrected two others SSO’s. 1/10/24
Sargent Road activation. Cause: Surcharging of system during wet weather.
5/24/24 30 Phillips Road. Cause: Grease build up which restricted flow.

A collapsed section of sewer main on Longview Drive was found during the Sewer
Departments video inspection program. This sewer was immediately repaired and
prevented a sanitary sewer overflow. The purchase of a sewer video camera and
certification of sewer employees in video inspections is a new tool that has been
successful in keeping the sewers flowing and identify issues prior to a complete
blockage of the system.

In 2024, the Sewer Department responded to 103 emergency dig safes requests. This
requires marking out all sewer infrastructure in the requested area within 1 hour.
Requests are received 24 hours a day/ 7 days a week.

      REPORT OF THE SOUTH ESSEX SEWERAGE DISTRICT

The South Essex Sewerage District facility and staff provide the region essential
wastewater treatment, keeping our waterways clean and protecting public health.
The treatment facility fulfilled its mission throughout 2024 and performed these
services to the Town of Marblehead within the approved budgeted amount. The
Marblehead Water and Sewer Commission would like to recognize the employees of
SESD and commend them for their dedication to the member communities.

SESD launched a new website in 2024, it is an excellent source for information on
the treatment process, the districts history, assets, planned and current projects and
community involvement. https://www.sesd.com/.

SESD is subject to Proposition 2 ½, with recent significant price increases, planned
rehabilitation work, and the emergency cured-in-place pipeline repair, there are real
budget challenges ahead. To prepare for forecasted budget increases that are over 2
½ percent, SESD has prepared a Centennial Plan which identifies the capital
improvements required to keep the treatment plant running efficiently, locates
advantageous funding sources, creates a project schedule to limit budget increases.

SESD and The Marblehead Water and Sewer Commission are committed to
protecting our surrounding waterways and the environment while providing
outstanding service to the residents of Marblehead.

I would be pleased to answer any questions concerning the Town’s wastewater
collection system or the related SESD facilities.


Respectfully submitted,

Amy McHugh
SESD Board Representative

                    ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
The Zoning Board of Appeals met in eleven sessions in 2024 from January to
November, via Zoom, at which it took the following actions: approved 67 and denied
1 special permit request, granted 1 extension request, allowed the withdrawal of 3
applications without prejudice, denied 1 administrative request for modifications.

Board members were Alan Lipkind, William Barlow, Leon Drachman, Benjamin
Labrecque, and William Moriarty. The alternates were Bruce Krasker, William
Rudolph, Christopher Casey, Marc Liebman. Special thanks to Lisa Lyons and Town
Planner, Alex Eitler.

Special thanks to Rebecca Curran Cutting for her guidance and friendship to the
Board over many years of service to the Town. We are so much better able to serve
the Town because of her selflessness and diligence.

Respectfully submitted,
William R. Moriarty
Chair

                             REGULAR MEETINGS
The regular scheduled meetings of the School Committee are held in the High School
Library bi-monthly at 7:00 p.m. except during the months of July and August. Since
the summer of 2019, recordings of the regularly scheduled School Committee
meetings have been available to review at a later time via the School Committee
website. The meetings are also televised to watch at a later date through MHTV.

               At the foundation of all district-wide planning and goal setting spanning the full
               operations of the department, were the input, creation and adoption of the Mission
               Statement and Vision Statement, culminating in the release of the Strategic Plan, the
               Planning for Success Initiative (PfS). The School Department’s annual objectives and
               Strategic Plan seeks to reflect the priorities identified in the district’s defined mission,
               vision, and goals, with the assumption of concurrently providing sufficient resources
               to ensure compliance of all federal, state and locally mandated educational programs
               and requirements while enabling the more detailed work in specific operational areas
               to occur with a common guide driving the output.

                              Central Administration
The start of the 2023-2024 school year opened with two days of professional
development for staff which began on August 29th. The first day of the 2023-2024
school year started on September 5th for grades 1st through 12th and Marblehead’s
youngest learners from Prekindergarten and Kindergarten were welcomed on
September 7th. The primary goal of the 2023-2024 school year was maintaining a
safe, inclusive and exemplary learning environment for all learners. The partnership
with METCO Headquarters continued to address equity and inclusion throughout the
district. Additionally, during the 2023-2024 school year, the Facilities and
Technology Department continued to work together on various projects to increase
the safety and security of Marblehead’s buildings and grounds.


                       The Office of Teaching and Learning
The goal of the Marblehead Public Schools Office of Teaching and Learning is to
fully align teaching and learning, grades preschool through twelve, within a multi-
tiered systems of support framework to ensure all students meet or exceed academic
and social-emotional learning expectations. Under new leadership for the 2023-2024
school year, the Office of Teaching and Learning focused on ensuring students and
staff had the resources needed to refine, broaden, and deepen curriculum and
instruction alignment within Marblehead Public Schools. A major focus for the
school year was the integration of a new literacy program for students in kindergarten
through grade 6. Additionally, the leadership team and instructional coaches
continued to work collaboratively with educators to plan professional development,
which allowed for alignment among our Professional Learning Communities in
support of instructional excellence and student achievement.

During the school year, the Student Services Department focused on professional
development opportunities that spanned the duration of the year. Throughout the first
part of the school year, virtual information sessions were regularly held by the
Director of Student Services and parents and guardians were encouraged to
participate. The Student Services Department continued to collaborate with other




                                       279
                 MARBLEHEAD TOWN REPORT 2024
 __________________________________________________________________

administrative leaders and stakeholders such as the Special Education Parent
Advisory Council (SEPAC) to discuss bringing improvements to the Department and
the overall testing and evaluation process.

The Lucretia and Jospeh Brown Elementary School and the Glover Elementary
School serves students in grades Prekindergarten through grade 3. The total number
of students enrolled at the Lucretia and Jospeh Brown School for the year ending June
of 2024 was 443. The total number of students enrolled at the Glover School for the
year ending June of 2024 was 321.

The Village Elementary School serves students in grades 4 through 6. The total
number of students enrolled at the Village School for the year ending June of 2024
was 532.

The Veterans Middle School Serves students in grades 7 and 8. The total number of
students enrolled at the Veterans Middle School for the year ending June of 2024 was
435.

The High School serves students in grades 9 through 12. The High School also serves
a population of postgraduate students. The total number of students enrolled at the
High School for the year ending June of 2024 was 1,074.
